<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">This article is about the 18th-century Methodist leader. For other people named John Wesley, see <a href="/wiki/John_Wesley_(disambiguation)" class="mw-disambig" title="John Wesley (disambiguation)">John Wesley (disambiguation)</a>.</div>

<span class="noviewer" typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:P_christianity.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="icon" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/P_christianity.svg/16px-P_christianity.svg.png" decoding="async" width="16" height="14" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/P_christianity.svg/24px-P_christianity.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/P_christianity.svg/32px-P_christianity.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="400" data-file-height="360" /></a></span>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Portal:Christianity" title="Portal:Christianity">Christianity&#32;portal</a>
</p>
<span class="noviewer" typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/John_Wesley_clipped.png/13px-John_Wesley_clipped.png" decoding="async" width="13" height="16" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/John_Wesley_clipped.png/19px-John_Wesley_clipped.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/John_Wesley_clipped.png/26px-John_Wesley_clipped.png 2x" data-file-width="283" data-file-height="349" /></span></span>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Portal:Methodism" title="Portal:Methodism">Methodism&#32;portal</a></td></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Methodism" title="Template:Methodism"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Methodism" title="Template talk:Methodism"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Methodism&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>John Wesley</b> (<span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;w&#39; in &#39;wind&#39;">w</span><span title="/ɛ/: &#39;e&#39; in &#39;dress&#39;">ɛ</span><span title="&#39;s&#39; in &#39;sigh&#39;">s</span><span title="&#39;l&#39; in &#39;lie&#39;">l</span><span title="/i/: &#39;y&#39; in &#39;happy&#39;">i</span></span>/</a></span></span>;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWells2008Wesley_1-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWells2008Wesley-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> 28 June &#160;&#91;<a href="/wiki/Old_Style_and_New_Style_dates" title="Old Style and New Style dates">O.S.</a> 17 June&#93;&#160;1703&#160;&#8211;&#32;2 March 1791) was an English <a href="/wiki/Cleric" class="mw-redirect" title="Cleric">cleric</a>, <a href="/wiki/Christian_theology" title="Christian theology">theologian</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Evangelism" title="Evangelism">evangelist</a> who was a leader of a <a href="/wiki/Christian_revival" title="Christian revival">revival</a> movement within the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Church of England</a> known as <a href="/wiki/Methodism" title="Methodism">Methodism</a>. The societies he founded became the dominant form of the independent Methodist movement that continues to this day.
</p><p>Educated at <a href="/wiki/Charterhouse_School" title="Charterhouse School">Charterhouse</a> and <a href="/wiki/Christ_Church,_Oxford" title="Christ Church, Oxford">Christ Church, Oxford</a>, Wesley was elected a <a href="/wiki/Fellow" title="Fellow">fellow</a> of <a href="/wiki/Lincoln_College,_Oxford" title="Lincoln College, Oxford">Lincoln College, Oxford</a>, in 1726 and <a href="/wiki/Ordination" title="Ordination">ordained</a> as an <a href="/wiki/Anglican" class="mw-redirect" title="Anglican">Anglican</a> priest two years later. At Oxford, he led the "<a href="/wiki/Holy_Club" title="Holy Club">Holy Club</a>", a society formed for the purpose of the study and the pursuit of a devout Christian life. After an unsuccessful two year ministry in Savannah, Georgia, he returned to London and joined a religious society led by <a href="/wiki/Moravian_Church" title="Moravian Church">Moravian Christians</a>. On 24 May 1738, he experienced what has come to be called his <a href="/wiki/Evangelical" class="mw-redirect" title="Evangelical">evangelical</a> conversion. He subsequently left the Moravians and began his own ministry.
</p><p>A key step in the development of Wesley's ministry was to travel and <a href="/wiki/Open-air_preaching" title="Open-air preaching">preach outdoors</a>, embracing <a href="/wiki/Arminianism" title="Arminianism">Arminian doctrines</a>. Moving across Great Britain and Ireland, he helped form and organise small Christian groups (societies) that developed intensive and personal accountability, <a href="/wiki/Discipleship" class="mw-redirect" title="Discipleship">discipleship</a>, and religious instruction. He appointed <a href="/wiki/Itinerant_preacher" title="Itinerant preacher">itinerant</a>, unordained evangelists—both women and men—to care for these groups of people. Under Wesley's direction, Methodists became leaders in many social issues of the day, including the <a href="/wiki/Abolitionism_in_the_United_Kingdom" title="Abolitionism in the United Kingdom">abolition of slavery</a> and support for women preachers.
</p><p>Although he was not a <a href="/wiki/Systematic_theology" title="Systematic theology">systematic theologian</a>, Wesley argued for the notion of <a href="/wiki/Christian_perfection" title="Christian perfection">Christian perfection</a> and against <a href="/wiki/Calvinism" title="Calvinism">Calvinism</a>. His evangelicalism, firmly grounded in <a href="/wiki/Sacrament" title="Sacrament">sacramental</a> theology, maintained that <a href="/wiki/Means_of_grace" title="Means of grace">means of grace</a> sometimes had a role in <a href="/wiki/Sanctification" class="mw-redirect" title="Sanctification">sanctification</a> of the believer; however, he taught that it was by <a href="/wiki/Faith_in_Christianity" title="Faith in Christianity">faith</a> a believer was transformed into the likeness of <a href="/wiki/Christ" class="mw-redirect" title="Christ">Christ</a>. He held that, in this life, Christians could achieve a state where the <a href="/wiki/Love_of_God_in_Christianity" title="Love of God in Christianity">love of God</a> "reigned supreme in their hearts", giving them not only <a href="/wiki/Outward_holiness" title="Outward holiness">outward</a> but inward holiness. Wesley's teachings, collectively known as <a href="/wiki/Wesleyan_theology" title="Wesleyan theology">Wesleyan theology</a>, continue to inform the doctrine of Methodist churches.
</p><p>Throughout his life, Wesley remained within the <a href="/wiki/Established_church" class="mw-redirect" title="Established church">established</a> Church of England, insisting that the Methodist movement lay well within its tradition.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThorsen200597_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThorsen200597-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> In his early ministry years, Wesley was barred from preaching in many parish churches and the Methodists were persecuted; he later became widely respected, and by the end of his life, was described as "the best-loved man in England".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKiefer2019_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKiefer2019-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r886046785">.mw-parser-output .toclimit-2 .toclevel-1 ul,.mw-parser-output .toclimit-3 .toclevel-2 ul,.mw-parser-output .toclimit-4 .toclevel-3 ul,.mw-parser-output .toclimit-5 .toclevel-4 ul,.mw-parser-output .toclimit-6 .toclevel-5 ul,.mw-parser-output .toclimit-7 .toclevel-6 ul{display:none}</style><div class="toclimit-3"><meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" /></div>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Wesley&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1096954695/mw-parser-output/.tmulti">.mw-parser-output .tmulti .multiimageinner{display:flex;flex-direction:column}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .trow{display:flex;flex-direction:row;clear:left;flex-wrap:wrap;width:100%;box-sizing:border-box}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .tsingle{margin:1px;float:left}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .theader{clear:both;font-weight:bold;text-align:center;align-self:center;background-color:transparent;width:100%}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .thumbcaption{background-color:transparent}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .text-align-left{text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .text-align-right{text-align:right}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .text-align-center{text-align:center}@media all and (max-width:720px){.mw-parser-output .tmulti .thumbinner{width:100%!important;box-sizing:border-box;max-width:none!important;align-items:center}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .trow{justify-content:center}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .tsingle{float:none!important;max-width:100%!important;box-sizing:border-box;text-align:center}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .tsingle .thumbcaption{text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .trow>.thumbcaption{text-align:center}}</style><div class="thumb tmulti tleft"><div class="thumbinner multiimageinner" style="width:104px;max-width:104px"><div class="trow"><div class="tsingle" style="width:102px;max-width:102px"><div class="thumbimage"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Samuelwesley.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/aa/Samuelwesley.jpg/100px-Samuelwesley.jpg" decoding="async" width="100" height="127" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/aa/Samuelwesley.jpg/150px-Samuelwesley.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/aa/Samuelwesley.jpg/200px-Samuelwesley.jpg 2x" data-file-width="751" data-file-height="952" /></a></span></div><div class="thumbcaption"><a href="/wiki/Samuel_Wesley_(poet)" class="mw-redirect" title="Samuel Wesley (poet)">Samuel Wesley</a></div></div></div><div class="trow"><div class="tsingle" style="width:102px;max-width:102px"><div class="thumbimage"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Susanna_Wesley.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/Susanna_Wesley.jpg/100px-Susanna_Wesley.jpg" decoding="async" width="100" height="127" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/Susanna_Wesley.jpg/150px-Susanna_Wesley.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/Susanna_Wesley.jpg/200px-Susanna_Wesley.jpg 2x" data-file-width="318" data-file-height="403" /></a></span></div><div class="thumbcaption"><a href="/wiki/Susanna_Wesley" title="Susanna Wesley">Susanna Wesley</a></div></div></div></div></div>
<p>John Wesley was born on 28 June &#160;&#91;<a href="/wiki/Old_Style_and_New_Style_dates" title="Old Style and New Style dates">O.S.</a> 17 June&#93;&#160;1703 in <a href="/wiki/Epworth,_Lincolnshire" title="Epworth, Lincolnshire">Epworth</a>, 23 miles (37&#160;km) north-west of <a href="/wiki/Lincoln,_England" title="Lincoln, England">Lincoln</a>. He was the fifteenth child of <a href="/wiki/Samuel_Wesley_(poet)" class="mw-redirect" title="Samuel Wesley (poet)">Samuel Wesley</a> and his wife <a href="/wiki/Susanna_Wesley" title="Susanna Wesley">Susanna Wesley</a> (née Annesley).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBBC_Humberside2008_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBBC_Humberside2008-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> Samuel Wesley was a graduate of the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Oxford" title="University of Oxford">University of Oxford</a> and a poet who, from 1696, was <a href="/wiki/Rector_(ecclesiastical)" title="Rector (ecclesiastical)">rector</a> of Epworth. He married Susanna, the twenty-fifth child of <a href="/wiki/Samuel_Annesley" title="Samuel Annesley">Samuel Annesley</a>, a <a href="/wiki/English_Dissenters" title="English Dissenters">dissenting</a> minister, in 1689. Ultimately, she bore nineteen children, of which nine lived beyond infancy. She and Samuel Wesley had become members of the Church of England as young adults.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERatcliffe2019_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERatcliffe2019-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As in many families at the time, Wesley's parents gave their children their early education. Each child, including the girls, was taught to read as soon as they turned five years old. They were expected to become proficient in <a href="/wiki/Latin" title="Latin">Latin</a> and <a href="/wiki/Greek_language" title="Greek language">Greek</a> and to have learned major portions of the <a href="/wiki/New_Testament" title="New Testament">New Testament</a> by heart. Susanna Wesley examined each child before the midday meal and before evening prayers. The children were not allowed to eat between meals and were interviewed singly by their mother one evening each week for the purpose of intensive spiritual instruction. In 1714, at age 11, Wesley was sent to the <a href="/wiki/Charterhouse_School" title="Charterhouse School">Charterhouse School</a> in London (under the mastership of <a href="/wiki/John_King_(clergyman)" class="mw-redirect" title="John King (clergyman)">John King</a> from 1715), where he lived the studious, methodical and, for a while, religious life in which he had been trained at home.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJohnson2002_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJohnson2002-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:The_rescue_of_the_young_John_Wesley_from_the_burning_parsona_Wellcome_V0006839.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4d/The_rescue_of_the_young_John_Wesley_from_the_burning_parsona_Wellcome_V0006839.jpg/220px-The_rescue_of_the_young_John_Wesley_from_the_burning_parsona_Wellcome_V0006839.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4d/The_rescue_of_the_young_John_Wesley_from_the_burning_parsona_Wellcome_V0006839.jpg/330px-The_rescue_of_the_young_John_Wesley_from_the_burning_parsona_Wellcome_V0006839.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4d/The_rescue_of_the_young_John_Wesley_from_the_burning_parsona_Wellcome_V0006839.jpg/440px-The_rescue_of_the_young_John_Wesley_from_the_burning_parsona_Wellcome_V0006839.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3254" data-file-height="2440" /></a><figcaption>The rescue of the young John Wesley from the burning rectory. Mezzotint by <a href="/wiki/Samuel_William_Reynolds" title="Samuel William Reynolds">Samuel William Reynolds</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Apart from his disciplined upbringing, a <a href="/wiki/Rectory" class="mw-redirect" title="Rectory">rectory</a> fire which occurred on 9 February 1709, when Wesley was five years old, left an indelible impression. Some time after 11:00&#160;pm, the rectory roof caught on fire. Sparks falling on the children's beds and cries of "fire" from the street roused the Wesleys who managed to shepherd all their children out of the house except for John who was left stranded on an upper floor.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWallace1997_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWallace1997-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> With stairs aflame and the roof about to collapse, Wesley was lifted out of a window by a parishioner standing on another man's shoulders. Wesley later used the phrase, "a brand plucked out of the fire", quoting <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://bible.oremus.org/?passage=Zechariah%203:2&amp;version=nrsv">Zechariah 3:2</a>, to describe the incident.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWallace1997_7-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWallace1997-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> This childhood deliverance subsequently became part of the Wesley legend, attesting to his special destiny and extraordinary work. Wesley was also influenced by the reported haunting of <a href="/wiki/Old_Rectory,_Epworth" title="Old Rectory, Epworth">Epworth Rectory</a> between 1716 and 1717. The Wesley family reported frequently hearing noises and occasionally seeing apparitions which they believed were caused by a ghost called 'Old Jeffery'.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECrain2009114_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrain2009114-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Education">Education</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Wesley&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Education">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Christ_Church_Cathedral_Interior_2,_Oxford,_UK_-_Diliff.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f3/Christ_Church_Cathedral_Interior_2%2C_Oxford%2C_UK_-_Diliff.jpg/220px-Christ_Church_Cathedral_Interior_2%2C_Oxford%2C_UK_-_Diliff.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="173" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f3/Christ_Church_Cathedral_Interior_2%2C_Oxford%2C_UK_-_Diliff.jpg/330px-Christ_Church_Cathedral_Interior_2%2C_Oxford%2C_UK_-_Diliff.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f3/Christ_Church_Cathedral_Interior_2%2C_Oxford%2C_UK_-_Diliff.jpg/440px-Christ_Church_Cathedral_Interior_2%2C_Oxford%2C_UK_-_Diliff.jpg 2x" data-file-width="8000" data-file-height="6284" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Christ_Church,_Oxford" title="Christ Church, Oxford">Christ Church</a>, cathedral of the <a href="/wiki/Diocese_of_Oxford" title="Diocese of Oxford">Diocese of Oxford</a>, Wesley's college chapel and place of ordination</figcaption></figure>
<p>In June 1720, Wesley entered <a href="/wiki/Christ_Church,_Oxford" title="Christ Church, Oxford">Christ Church, Oxford</a>. After graduating in 1724, Wesley stayed on at Christ Church to study for his <a href="/wiki/Master%27s_degree" title="Master&#39;s degree">master's degree</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomkins200322_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomkins200322-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He was ordained a <a href="/wiki/Deacon" title="Deacon">deacon</a> on 25 September 1725—<a href="/wiki/Holy_orders" title="Holy orders">holy orders</a> being a necessary step toward becoming a <a href="/wiki/Fellow" title="Fellow">fellow</a> and tutor at the university.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEYoung201561_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEYoung201561-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> On 17 March 1726, Wesley was unanimously elected a fellow of <a href="/wiki/Lincoln_College,_Oxford" title="Lincoln College, Oxford">Lincoln College</a>, Oxford. This carried with it the right to a room at the college and regular salary.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomkins200327_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomkins200327-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> While continuing his studies, he taught <a href="/wiki/Greek_language" title="Greek language">Greek</a> and philosophy, lectured on the New Testament and moderated daily disputations at the university.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomkins200327_11-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomkins200327-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> However, a call to ministry intruded upon his academic career. In August 1727, after completing his master's degree, Wesley returned to Epworth. His father had requested his assistance in serving the neighbouring cure of <a href="/wiki/Wroot" title="Wroot">Wroot</a>. Ordained a priest on 22 September 1728,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEYoung201561_10-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEYoung201561-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> Wesley served as a parish <a href="/wiki/Curate" title="Curate">curate</a> for two years.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomkins200323_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomkins200323-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the year of his ordination he read <a href="/wiki/Thomas_%C3%A0_Kempis" title="Thomas à Kempis">Thomas à Kempis</a> and <a href="/wiki/Jeremy_Taylor" title="Jeremy Taylor">Jeremy Taylor</a>, showed his interest in <a href="/wiki/Christian_mysticism" title="Christian mysticism">mysticism</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWesleyWhaling198110_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWesleyWhaling198110-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> and began to seek the religious truths which underlay the great revival of the 18th century. The reading of <a href="/wiki/William_Law" title="William Law">William Law</a>'s <i>Christian Perfection</i> and <i>A Serious Call to a Devout and Holy Life</i> gave him, he said, a more sublime view of the law of God; and he resolved to keep it, inwardly and outwardly, as sacredly as possible, believing that in obedience he would find salvation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELaw2009_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELaw2009-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> He pursued a rigidly methodical and abstemious life, studied Scripture, and performed his religious duties diligently, depriving himself so that he would have alms to give. He began to seek after holiness of heart and life.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELaw2009_14-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELaw2009-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Wesley returned to Oxford in November 1729 at the request of the <a href="/wiki/Rector_(academia)" title="Rector (academia)">Rector</a> of Lincoln College and to maintain his status as junior fellow.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomkins200331_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomkins200331-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Holy_Club">Holy Club</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Wesley&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Holy Club">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Holy_Club" title="Holy Club">Holy Club</a></div>
<p>During Wesley's absence, his younger brother Charles (1707–88) matriculated at Christ Church. Along with two fellow students, he formed a small club for the purpose of study and the pursuit of a devout Christian life.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomkins200331_15-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomkins200331-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> On Wesley's return, he became the leader of the group which increased somewhat in number and greatly in commitment. The group met daily from six until nine for prayer, <a href="/wiki/Psalm" class="mw-redirect" title="Psalm">psalms</a>, and reading of the Greek <a href="/wiki/New_Testament" title="New Testament">New Testament</a>. They prayed every waking hour for several minutes and each day for a special virtue. While the church's prescribed attendance was only three times a year, they took <a href="/wiki/Eucharist" title="Eucharist">Communion</a> every Sunday. They <a href="/wiki/Fasting" title="Fasting">fasted</a> on Wednesdays and Fridays until <a href="/wiki/Nones_(liturgy)" title="Nones (liturgy)">nones</a> (3:00&#160;pm) as was commonly observed in the ancient church.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStoughton1878296_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStoughton1878296-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> In 1730, the group began the practice of visiting prisoners in <a href="/wiki/Gaol" class="mw-redirect" title="Gaol">gaol</a>. They preached, educated, and relieved gaoled debtors whenever possible, and cared for the sick.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEIovino2016_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEIovino2016-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Given the low ebb of spirituality in Oxford at that time, it was not surprising that Wesley's group provoked a negative reaction. They were considered to be religious "enthusiasts", which in the context of the time meant <a href="/wiki/Religious_fanaticism" title="Religious fanaticism">religious fanatics</a>. University wits styled them the "Holy Club", a title of derision. Currents of opposition became a furore following the mental breakdown and death of a group member, William Morgan.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomkins200337_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomkins200337-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> In response to the charge that "rigorous fasting" had hastened his death, Wesley noted that Morgan had left off fasting a year and a half since. In the same letter, which was widely circulated, Wesley referred to the name "Methodist" with which "some of our neighbors are pleased to compliment us."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWesley1931letter_1732_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWesley1931letter_1732-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> That name was used by an anonymous author in a published <a href="/wiki/Pamphlet" title="Pamphlet">pamphlet</a> (1732) describing Wesley and his group, "The Oxford Methodists".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThe_Methodist_Church2011_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_Methodist_Church2011-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> This ministry, however, was not without controversy. The Holy Club ministered and maintained support for Thomas Blair who in 1732 was found guilty for <a href="/wiki/Sodomy" title="Sodomy">sodomy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHeitzenrater1972393_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeitzenrater1972393-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> Blair was notorious among the townspeople and his fellow prisoners, and Wesley continued to support him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHeitzenrater1972392_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeitzenrater1972392-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>For all of his outward <a href="/wiki/Piety" title="Piety">piety</a>, Wesley sought to cultivate his inner holiness or at least his sincerity as evidence of being a true Christian. A list of "General Questions" which he developed in 1730 evolved into an elaborate grid by 1734 in which he recorded his daily activities hour-by-hour, resolutions he had broken or kept, and ranked his hourly "temper of devotion" on a scale of 1 to 9. Wesley also regarded the contempt with which he and his group were held to be a mark of a true Christian. As he put it in a letter to his father, "Till he be thus contemned, no man is in a state of salvation."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWesleyBenson1827108_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWesleyBenson1827108-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Journey_to_Savannah.2C_Georgia"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Journey_to_Savannah,_Georgia">Journey to Savannah, Georgia</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Wesley&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Journey to Savannah, Georgia">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:15-17-040,_John_Wesley_Statue_-_panoramio.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/27/15-17-040%2C_John_Wesley_Statue_-_panoramio.jpg/200px-15-17-040%2C_John_Wesley_Statue_-_panoramio.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="268" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/27/15-17-040%2C_John_Wesley_Statue_-_panoramio.jpg/300px-15-17-040%2C_John_Wesley_Statue_-_panoramio.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/27/15-17-040%2C_John_Wesley_Statue_-_panoramio.jpg/400px-15-17-040%2C_John_Wesley_Statue_-_panoramio.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1960" data-file-height="2631" /></a><figcaption>Statue of Wesley in <a href="/wiki/Savannah,_Georgia" title="Savannah, Georgia">Savannah, Georgia</a>, United States</figcaption></figure>
<p>On 14 October 1735, Wesley and his brother Charles sailed on <i>The Simmonds</i> from <a href="/wiki/Gravesend" title="Gravesend">Gravesend</a> in Kent for <a href="/wiki/Savannah,_Georgia" title="Savannah, Georgia">Savannah</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Province_of_Georgia" title="Province of Georgia">Province of Georgia</a> in the <a href="/wiki/British_America" title="British America">American colonies</a> at the request of <a href="/wiki/James_Oglethorpe" title="James Oglethorpe">James Oglethorpe</a>, who had founded the colony in 1733 on behalf of the <a href="/wiki/Trustees_for_the_Establishment_of_the_Colony_of_Georgia_in_America" title="Trustees for the Establishment of the Colony of Georgia in America">Trustees for the Establishment of the Colony of Georgia in America</a>. Oglethorpe wanted Wesley to be the minister of the newly formed <a href="/wiki/Christ_Church_(Savannah,_Georgia)" title="Christ Church (Savannah, Georgia)">Savannah parish</a>, a new town laid out in accordance with the famous <a href="/wiki/Oglethorpe_Plan" title="Oglethorpe Plan">Oglethorpe Plan</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERossStacey1998_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERossStacey1998-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>It was on the voyage to the colonies that the Wesleys first came into contact with <a href="/wiki/Moravian_Church" title="Moravian Church">Moravian</a> settlers. Wesley was influenced by their deep faith and spirituality rooted in <a href="/wiki/Pietism" title="Pietism">pietism</a>. At one point in the voyage a storm came up and broke the mast off the ship. While the English panicked, the Moravians calmly sang hymns and prayed. This experience led Wesley to believe that the Moravians possessed an inner strength which he lacked.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERossStacey1998_24-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERossStacey1998-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> The deeply personal religion that the Moravian pietists practiced heavily influenced Wesley and is reflected in his theology of Methodism.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERossStacey1998_24-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERossStacey1998-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Wesley_preaching_to_native_American_Indians._Engraving._Wellcome_V0006867.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e5/John_Wesley_preaching_to_native_American_Indians._Engraving._Wellcome_V0006867.jpg/280px-John_Wesley_preaching_to_native_American_Indians._Engraving._Wellcome_V0006867.jpg" decoding="async" width="280" height="204" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e5/John_Wesley_preaching_to_native_American_Indians._Engraving._Wellcome_V0006867.jpg/420px-John_Wesley_preaching_to_native_American_Indians._Engraving._Wellcome_V0006867.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e5/John_Wesley_preaching_to_native_American_Indians._Engraving._Wellcome_V0006867.jpg/560px-John_Wesley_preaching_to_native_American_Indians._Engraving._Wellcome_V0006867.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3336" data-file-height="2432" /></a><figcaption>An engraving, usually titled <i>John Wesley preaching to the <a href="/wiki/Native_Americans_in_the_United_States" title="Native Americans in the United States">Indians</a></i>, artist unattributed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENYPL_Digital_Collections_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENYPL_Digital_Collections-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>Wesley arrived in the colony in February 1736, and lived for a year at the <a href="/wiki/Parsonage" class="mw-redirect" title="Parsonage">parsonage</a> that stood on the site of today's <a href="/wiki/Oliver_Sturges_House" title="Oliver Sturges House">Oliver Sturges House</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-nrhp_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nrhp-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> He approached the Georgia mission as a <a href="/wiki/High_church" title="High church">High churchman</a>, seeing it as an opportunity to revive "<a href="/wiki/Early_Christianity" title="Early Christianity">primitive Christianity</a>" in a primitive environment.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHammond201413_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHammond201413-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> Although his primary goal was to evangelize the Native American people, a shortage of clergy in the colony largely limited his ministry to European settlers in Savannah. While his ministry has often been judged to have been a failure in comparison to his later success as a leader in the <a href="/wiki/Evangelical_Revival" class="mw-redirect" title="Evangelical Revival">Evangelical Revival</a>, Wesley gathered around him a group of devoted Christians, who met in a number of small group religious societies. At the same time, attendance at <a href="/wiki/Eucharist" title="Eucharist">Communion</a> increased over the course of nearly two years in which he served as <a href="/wiki/Christ_Church_(Savannah,_Georgia)" title="Christ Church (Savannah, Georgia)">Christ Church</a>'s parish priest.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHammond2014191_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHammond2014191-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Nonetheless, Wesley's High Church ministry was controversial among the colonists and it ended in disappointment after Wesley fell in love with a young woman named Sophia (or Sophy) Hopkey. He hesitated to marry her because he felt that his first priority in Georgia was to be a missionary to the Native Americans, and he was interested in the practice of <a href="/wiki/Clerical_celibacy" title="Clerical celibacy">clerical celibacy</a> within early Christianity.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHodges2014_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHodges2014-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> Following her marriage to William Williamson, Wesley believed Sophia's former zeal for practicing the Christian faith declined. In strictly applying the rubrics of the <i><a href="/wiki/Book_of_Common_Prayer" title="Book of Common Prayer">Book of Common Prayer</a></i>, Wesley denied her Communion after she failed to signify to him in advance her intention of taking it.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomkins200354–55_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomkins200354–55-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> As a result, legal proceedings against him ensued in which a clear resolution seemed unlikely. On 22 December 1737, Wesley fled the colony and returned to England.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>It has been widely recognized that one of the most significant accomplishments of Wesley's Georgia mission was his publication of a <i>Collection of Psalms and Hymns</i>. The <i>Collection</i> was the first Anglican <a href="/wiki/Hymnal" title="Hymnal">hymnal</a> published in America, and the first of many hymn-books Wesley published. It included five hymns he translated from German.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHammond2014106_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHammond2014106-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Wesley.27s_.22Aldersgate_experience.22"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Wesley's_&quot;Aldersgate_experience&quot;">Wesley's "Aldersgate experience"</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Wesley&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Wesley&#039;s &quot;Aldersgate experience&quot;">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1096954695/mw-parser-output/.tmulti"><div class="thumb tmulti tright"><div class="thumbinner multiimageinner" style="width:204px;max-width:204px"><div class="trow"><div class="theader">Aldersgate memorials</div></div><div class="trow"><div class="tsingle" style="width:202px;max-width:202px"><div class="thumbimage"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:The_probable_site_where_on_May_24_1738_John_Wesley_felt_his_heart_strangely_warmed._This_experience_of_grace_was_the_beginning_of_Methodism.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/The_probable_site_where_on_May_24_1738_John_Wesley_felt_his_heart_strangely_warmed._This_experience_of_grace_was_the_beginning_of_Methodism.jpg/200px-The_probable_site_where_on_May_24_1738_John_Wesley_felt_his_heart_strangely_warmed._This_experience_of_grace_was_the_beginning_of_Methodism.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="150" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/The_probable_site_where_on_May_24_1738_John_Wesley_felt_his_heart_strangely_warmed._This_experience_of_grace_was_the_beginning_of_Methodism.jpg/300px-The_probable_site_where_on_May_24_1738_John_Wesley_felt_his_heart_strangely_warmed._This_experience_of_grace_was_the_beginning_of_Methodism.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/The_probable_site_where_on_May_24_1738_John_Wesley_felt_his_heart_strangely_warmed._This_experience_of_grace_was_the_beginning_of_Methodism.jpg/400px-The_probable_site_where_on_May_24_1738_John_Wesley_felt_his_heart_strangely_warmed._This_experience_of_grace_was_the_beginning_of_Methodism.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2000" data-file-height="1500" /></a></span></div><div class="thumbcaption">Plaque erected in August 1926 at the probable site of the Altersgate experience by the <a href="/wiki/Drew_University" title="Drew University">Drew Theological Seminary</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Methodist_Episcopal_Church" title="Methodist Episcopal Church">Methodist Episcopal Church</a>.</div></div></div><div class="trow"><div class="tsingle" style="width:202px;max-width:202px"><div class="thumbimage"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Wesley_memorial_Aldersgate.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a8/John_Wesley_memorial_Aldersgate.jpg/200px-John_Wesley_memorial_Aldersgate.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="293" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a8/John_Wesley_memorial_Aldersgate.jpg/300px-John_Wesley_memorial_Aldersgate.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a8/John_Wesley_memorial_Aldersgate.jpg/400px-John_Wesley_memorial_Aldersgate.jpg 2x" data-file-width="958" data-file-height="1404" /></a></span></div><div class="thumbcaption">The "Aldersgate Flame" commemorates the event and features text from Wesley's journal describing his experience.</div></div></div></div></div>
<p>As a result of his experience in Georgia, Wesley became depressed. While on his voyage home to England, he had the opportunity to think about his own religious faith. He found that, although he had committed to the life of following Christ, he was dissatisfied with his spiritual soundness and felt inadequate to <a href="/wiki/Preaching" class="mw-redirect" title="Preaching">preach</a>, especially after witnessing the confident way in which the <a href="/wiki/Moravian_Church" title="Moravian Church">Moravians</a> had preached their faith. Both he and Charles received counsel from Moravian minister <a href="/wiki/Peter_Boehler" title="Peter Boehler">Peter Boehler</a>, who was temporarily in England awaiting permission to depart for Georgia himself. Boehler encouraged Wesley to "preach faith until you have it".<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>Wesley's noted "Aldersgate experience" of 24 May 1738, at a Moravian meeting in <a href="/wiki/Aldersgate_Street" class="mw-redirect" title="Aldersgate Street">Aldersgate Street</a>, London, in which he heard a reading of <a href="/wiki/Martin_Luther" title="Martin Luther">Martin Luther</a>'s preface to the <a href="/wiki/Epistle_to_the_Romans" title="Epistle to the Romans">Epistle to the Romans</a>, revolutionised the character and method of his ministry.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHurst1903102–103_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHurst1903102–103-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> The previous week he had been highly impressed by the sermon of <a href="/wiki/John_Heylyn" title="John Heylyn">John Heylyn</a>, whom he was assisting in the service at <a href="/wiki/St_Mary_le_Strand" title="St Mary le Strand">St Mary le Strand</a>. Earlier that day, he had heard the choir at <a href="/wiki/St_Paul%27s_Cathedral" title="St Paul&#39;s Cathedral">St Paul's Cathedral</a> singing <a href="/wiki/Psalm_130" title="Psalm 130">Psalm 130</a>, where the Psalmist calls to God "Out of the depths."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWesley2000a_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWesley2000a-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>But it was still a depressed Wesley who attended a service on the evening of 24 May. Wesley recounted his Aldersgate experience in his journal: 
</p>
<blockquote><p>"In the evening I went very unwillingly to a society in Aldersgate Street, where one was reading Luther's Preface to the Epistle to the Romans. About a quarter before nine, while he was describing the change which God works in the heart through faith in Christ, I felt my heart strangely warmed. I felt I did trust in Christ, Christ alone for salvation, and an assurance was given me that he had taken away my sins, even mine, and saved me from the law of sin and death."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDreyer199927_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDreyer199927-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>A few weeks later, Wesley preached a sermon on the doctrine of personal salvation by faith,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWesleyJackson1979Sermon_#1:_Salvation_By_Faith_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWesleyJackson1979Sermon_#1:_Salvation_By_Faith-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> which was followed by another, on God's <a href="/wiki/Grace_(Christianity)" class="mw-redirect" title="Grace (Christianity)">grace</a> "free in all, and free for all."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWesleyJackson1979Sermon_#128:_Free_Grace_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWesleyJackson1979Sermon_#128:_Free_Grace-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> Considered a pivotal moment, Daniel L. Burnett writes: "The significance of Wesley's Aldersgate Experience is monumental ... Without it, the names of Wesley and Methodism would likely be nothing more than obscure footnotes in the pages of church history."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBurnett200636_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurnett200636-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> Burnett describes this event as Wesley's "Evangelical Conversion".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBurnett200636–37_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurnett200636–37-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> May 24 is commemorated in Methodist churches as <a href="/wiki/Aldersgate_Day" title="Aldersgate Day">Aldersgate Day</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWaltz1991_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWaltz1991-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="After_Aldersgate:_Working_with_the_Moravians">After Aldersgate: Working with the Moravians</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Wesley&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: After Aldersgate: Working with the Moravians">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Wesley_preaching_on_his_fathers_grave-_in_the_church_yard_at_Epworth_Sunday_June_6th_1742_LCCN2002707689.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/John_Wesley_preaching_on_his_fathers_grave-_in_the_church_yard_at_Epworth_Sunday_June_6th_1742_LCCN2002707689.jpg/220px-John_Wesley_preaching_on_his_fathers_grave-_in_the_church_yard_at_Epworth_Sunday_June_6th_1742_LCCN2002707689.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="159" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/John_Wesley_preaching_on_his_fathers_grave-_in_the_church_yard_at_Epworth_Sunday_June_6th_1742_LCCN2002707689.jpg/330px-John_Wesley_preaching_on_his_fathers_grave-_in_the_church_yard_at_Epworth_Sunday_June_6th_1742_LCCN2002707689.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/John_Wesley_preaching_on_his_fathers_grave-_in_the_church_yard_at_Epworth_Sunday_June_6th_1742_LCCN2002707689.jpg/440px-John_Wesley_preaching_on_his_fathers_grave-_in_the_church_yard_at_Epworth_Sunday_June_6th_1742_LCCN2002707689.jpg 2x" data-file-width="5829" data-file-height="4219" /></a><figcaption>When forbidden from preaching from the pulpits of <a href="/wiki/Parish_church" title="Parish church">parish churches</a>, Wesley began open-air preaching.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Wesley allied himself with the Moravian society in Fetter Lane. In August 1738 Wesley travelled to Germany, specifically to see <a href="/wiki/Herrnhut" title="Herrnhut">Herrnhut</a> in <a href="/wiki/Electorate_of_Saxony" title="Electorate of Saxony">Saxony</a>, as he wished to study at the Moravian headquarters there.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDose2015_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDose2015-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> On his return to England, Wesley drew up rules for the "bands" into which the <a href="/wiki/Fetter_Lane_Society" title="Fetter Lane Society">Fetter Lane Society</a> was divided and published a collection of <a href="/wiki/Hymn" title="Hymn">hymns</a> for them.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWesley1831192_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWesley1831192-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> He met frequently with this and other religious societies in London but did not preach often in 1738, because most of the <a href="/wiki/Parish_church" title="Parish church">parish churches</a> were closed to him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGreen2019_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGreen2019-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Wesley's Oxford friend, the evangelist <a href="/wiki/George_Whitefield" title="George Whitefield">George Whitefield</a>, was also excluded from the churches of <a href="/wiki/Bristol" title="Bristol">Bristol</a> upon his return from America. When Wesley reached Bristol, the city was booming with new industrial and commercial development.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> Because of this, there were social uproars with riots and religious troubles.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_45-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> Going to the neighbouring village of <a href="/wiki/Kingswood,_South_Gloucestershire" title="Kingswood, South Gloucestershire">Kingswood</a>, in February 1739, Whitefield preached in the open air to a company of miners.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELAMC2019_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELAMC2019-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> Later he preached in <a href="/wiki/Whitefield%27s_Tabernacle,_Kingswood" title="Whitefield&#39;s Tabernacle, Kingswood">Whitefield's Tabernacle</a>. Wesley hesitated to accept Whitefield's call to copy this bold step. Overcoming his scruples, he preached the first time at Whitefield's invitation a <a href="/wiki/Open-air_preaching" title="Open-air preaching">sermon in the open air</a>, near Bristol, in April 1739. Wesley wrote,
</p>
<blockquote><p>
I could scarce reconcile myself to this strange way of preaching in the fields, of which he [Whitefield] set me an example on Sunday; having been all my life till very lately so tenacious of every point relating to decency and order, that I should have thought the saving of souls almost a sin if it had not been done in a church.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBuckley1898_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBuckley1898-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Wesley was unhappy about the idea of field preaching as he believed Anglican <a href="/wiki/Liturgy" title="Liturgy">liturgy</a> had much to offer in its practice. Earlier in his life he would have thought that such a method of saving souls was "almost a sin."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETomkins200369_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETomkins200369-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> He recognised the open-air services were successful in reaching men and women who would not enter most churches. From then on he took the opportunities to preach wherever an assembly could be brought together, more than once using his father's tombstone at Epworth as a <a href="/wiki/Pulpit" title="Pulpit">pulpit</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBBC2011_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBBC2011-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrownlow1859_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrownlow1859-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> Wesley preached to create repentance, prayed for conversion, dealt with hysterical behavior, and preached to upwards of thousands through field preaching.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_45-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> Wesley continued for fifty years—entering churches when he was invited, and taking his stand in the fields, in halls, cottages, and chapels, when the churches would not receive him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBBC2011_49-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBBC2011-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Late in 1739 Wesley broke with the Moravians in London. Wesley had helped them organise the Fetter Lane Society, and those converted by his preaching and that of his brother and Whitefield had become members of their bands. But he believed they fell into <a href="/wiki/Heresy" title="Heresy">heresy</a> by supporting <a href="/wiki/Quietism_(Christian_philosophy)" class="mw-redirect" title="Quietism (Christian philosophy)">quietism</a>, so he decided to form his own followers into a separate society.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith20102_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith20102-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> "Thus," he wrote, "without any previous plan, began the Methodist Society in England."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOden2013_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOden2013-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> He soon formed similar societies in Bristol and Kingswood, and Wesley and his friends made converts wherever they went.
</p><p>Wesley traveled to Ireland for the first time in 1747 and continued through 1789.<sup id="cite_ref-:03_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:03-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> He rejected the Ireland Catholic Church, so he worked to convert the people of Ireland to Methodism.<sup id="cite_ref-:03_53-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:03-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> Overall, the numbers grew to over 15,000 by 1795.<sup id="cite_ref-:03_53-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:03-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Persecutions_and_lay_preaching">Persecutions and lay preaching</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Wesley&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Persecutions and lay preaching">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>From 1739 onward, Wesley and the Methodists were persecuted by clergy and religious magistrates for various reasons.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETooley2014_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETooley2014-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> Though Wesley had been ordained an Anglican priest, many other Methodist leaders had not received <a href="/wiki/Ordination" title="Ordination">ordination</a>. And for his own part, Wesley flouted many regulations of the Church of England concerning parish boundaries and who had authority to preach.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELane2015_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELane2015-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> This was seen as a social threat that disregarded institutions. Clergy attacked them in sermons and in print, and at times mobs attacked them. Wesley and his followers continued to work among the neglected and needy. They were denounced as promulgators of strange doctrines, fomenters of religious disturbances; as blind fanatics, leading people astray, claiming miraculous gifts, attacking the clergy of the Church of England, and trying to re-establish <a href="/wiki/Catholicism" class="mw-redirect" title="Catholicism">Catholicism</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELane2015_55-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELane2015-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Wesley felt that the church failed to call sinners to <a href="/wiki/Repentance" title="Repentance">repentance</a>, that many of the clergy were corrupt, and that people were perishing in their sins. He believed he was commissioned by God to bring about <a href="/wiki/Christian_revival" title="Christian revival">revival</a> in the church, and no opposition, persecution, or obstacles could prevail against the divine urgency and authority of this commission. The prejudices of his High-church training, his strict notions of the methods and proprieties of public worship, his views of the <a href="/wiki/Apostolic_succession" title="Apostolic succession">apostolic succession</a> and the prerogatives of the priest, even his most cherished convictions, were not allowed to stand in the way.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBowen1901_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBowen1901-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Seeing that he and the few clergy co-operating with him could not do the work that needed to be done, Wesley was led, as early as 1739, to approve <a href="/wiki/Methodist_local_preacher" title="Methodist local preacher">local preachers</a>. He evaluated and approved men who were not ordained by the Anglican Church to preach and do pastoral work. This expansion of <a href="/wiki/Laity" title="Laity">lay</a> preachers was one of the keys of the growth of Methodism.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBurdon200523_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurdon200523-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Chapels_and_organisations">Chapels and organisations</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Wesley&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Chapels and organisations">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1096954695/mw-parser-output/.tmulti"><div class="thumb tmulti tright"><div class="thumbinner multiimageinner" style="width:392px;max-width:392px"><div class="trow"><div class="tsingle" style="width:189px;max-width:189px"><div class="thumbimage" style="height:131px;overflow:hidden"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:The_Foundery.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/c/c7/The_Foundery.jpg/187px-The_Foundery.jpg" decoding="async" width="187" height="132" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/c/c7/The_Foundery.jpg/281px-The_Foundery.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/c/c7/The_Foundery.jpg/374px-The_Foundery.jpg 2x" data-file-width="884" data-file-height="623" /></a></span></div><div class="thumbcaption">The first Methodist chapel, called "<a href="/wiki/The_Foundery" title="The Foundery">The Foundery</a>"</div></div><div class="tsingle" style="width:199px;max-width:199px"><div class="thumbimage" style="height:131px;overflow:hidden"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Wesley%27s_Chapel,_Methodist_church,_London.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/41/Wesley%27s_Chapel%2C_Methodist_church%2C_London.jpg/197px-Wesley%27s_Chapel%2C_Methodist_church%2C_London.jpg" decoding="async" width="197" height="131" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/41/Wesley%27s_Chapel%2C_Methodist_church%2C_London.jpg/296px-Wesley%27s_Chapel%2C_Methodist_church%2C_London.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/41/Wesley%27s_Chapel%2C_Methodist_church%2C_London.jpg/394px-Wesley%27s_Chapel%2C_Methodist_church%2C_London.jpg 2x" data-file-width="6000" data-file-height="4000" /></a></span></div><div class="thumbcaption"><a href="/wiki/Wesley%27s_Chapel" title="Wesley&#39;s Chapel">Wesley's Chapel</a>, originally known as "City Road Chapel"</div></div></div></div></div>
<p>As his societies needed houses to worship in, Wesley began to provide chapels, first in Bristol at the <a href="/wiki/New_Room,_Bristol" class="mw-redirect" title="New Room, Bristol">New Room</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThe_New_Room2017_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_New_Room2017-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> then in London (first <a href="/wiki/The_Foundery" title="The Foundery">The Foundery</a> and then <a href="/wiki/Wesley%27s_Chapel" title="Wesley&#39;s Chapel">Wesley's Chapel</a>) and elsewhere. The Foundery was an early chapel used by Wesley.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThe_Asbury_Triptych2019_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_Asbury_Triptych2019-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> The location of the Foundery is shown on an 18th-century map, where it rests between Tabernacle Street and Worship Street in the <a href="/wiki/Moorfields" title="Moorfields">Moorfields</a> area of London. When the Wesleys spotted the building atop Windmill Hill, north of <a href="/wiki/Finsbury_Fields" class="mw-redirect" title="Finsbury Fields">Finsbury Fields</a>, the structure which previously cast brass guns and mortars for the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Ordnance" title="Royal Ordnance">Royal Ordnance</a> had been sitting vacant for 23 years; it had been abandoned because of an explosion on 10 May 1716.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETooley187048_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETooley187048-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Bristol chapel (built in 1739) was at first in the hands of trustees. A large debt was contracted, and Wesley's friends urged him to keep it under his own control, so the deed was cancelled and he became sole trustee.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBates1983_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBates1983-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> Following this precedent, all Methodist chapels were committed in trust to him until by a "deed of declaration", all his interests in them were transferred to a body of preachers called the "Legal Hundred".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHurst1903Chapter_XVIII_–_Setting_His_House_in_Order_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHurst1903Chapter_XVIII_–_Setting_His_House_in_Order-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>When disorder arose among some members of the societies, Wesley adopted giving tickets to members, with their names written by his own hand. These were renewed every three months. Those deemed unworthy did not receive new tickets and dropped out of the society without disturbance. The tickets were regarded as commendatory letters.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEParkinson1898_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEParkinson1898-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:WesleyStatue.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/WesleyStatue.jpg/170px-WesleyStatue.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="287" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/WesleyStatue.jpg/255px-WesleyStatue.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/WesleyStatue.jpg/340px-WesleyStatue.jpg 2x" data-file-width="909" data-file-height="1532" /></a><figcaption>Statue of Wesley on horseback, in the courtyard of the "<a href="/wiki/New_Room,_Bristol" class="mw-redirect" title="New Room, Bristol">New Room</a>" chapel in <a href="/wiki/Bristol" title="Bristol">Bristol</a>. Bristol was Wesley's base for much of the 1740s and 1750s.</figcaption></figure>
<p>When the debt on a chapel became a burden, it was proposed that one in 12 members should collect offerings regularly from the 11 allotted to him. Out of this grew the Methodist class-meeting system in 1742. To keep the disorderly out of the societies, Wesley established a probationary system. He undertook to visit each society regularly in what became the quarterly visitation, or conference. As the number of societies increased, Wesley could not keep personal contact, so in 1743 he drew up a set of "General Rules" for the "United Societies".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHurst1903Chapter_IX_–_Society_and_Class_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHurst1903Chapter_IX_–_Society_and_Class-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> These were the nucleus of the Methodist <i><a href="/wiki/Book_of_Discipline_(United_Methodist)" title="Book of Discipline (United Methodist)">Discipline</a></i>, still the basis of modern Methodism.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPatterson198477_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPatterson198477-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Wesley laid the foundations of what now constitutes the <a href="/wiki/Organisation_of_the_Methodist_Church_of_Great_Britain" title="Organisation of the Methodist Church of Great Britain">organisation of the Methodist Church</a>. Over time, a shifting pattern of societies, circuits, quarterly meetings, annual conferences, classes, bands, and select societies took shape.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHurst1903Chapter_IX_–_Society_and_Class_64-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHurst1903Chapter_IX_–_Society_and_Class-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> At the local level, there were numerous societies of different sizes which were grouped into circuits to which <a href="/wiki/Itinerant_preacher" title="Itinerant preacher">travelling preachers</a> were appointed for two-year periods. Circuit officials met quarterly under a senior travelling preacher or "assistant." Conferences with Wesley, travelling preachers and others were convened annually for the purpose of co-ordinating doctrine and discipline for the entire connection. Classes of a dozen or so society members under a leader met weekly for spiritual fellowship and guidance. In early years, there were "bands" of the spiritually gifted who consciously pursued perfection. Those who were regarded to have achieved it were grouped in select societies or bands. In 1744, there were 77 such members. There also was a <a href="/wiki/Penitent_band" title="Penitent band">category of penitents</a> which consisted of <a href="/wiki/Backslider" class="mw-redirect" title="Backslider">backsliders</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHurst1903Chapter_IX_–_Society_and_Class_64-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHurst1903Chapter_IX_–_Society_and_Class-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Wesley%27s_House.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8f/Wesley%27s_House.jpg/220px-Wesley%27s_House.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8f/Wesley%27s_House.jpg/330px-Wesley%27s_House.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8f/Wesley%27s_House.jpg/440px-Wesley%27s_House.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3888" data-file-height="2592" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/John_Wesley%27s_House" class="mw-redirect" title="John Wesley&#39;s House">Wesley's house</a>, next to Wesley's Chapel, <a href="/wiki/City_Road" title="City Road">City Road</a>, London</figcaption></figure>
<p>As the number of preachers and preaching-houses increased, doctrinal and administrative matters needed to be discussed; so John and Charles Wesley, along with four other clergy and four lay preachers, met for consultation in London in 1744. This was the first Methodist conference; subsequently, the Conference (with Wesley as its president) became the ruling body of the Methodist movement.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGraves2007_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGraves2007-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> Two years later, to help preachers work more systematically and societies receive services more regularly, Wesley appointed "helpers" to definitive <a href="/wiki/Methodist_Circuit" class="mw-redirect" title="Methodist Circuit">circuits</a>. Each circuit included at least 30 appointments a month. Believing that the preacher's efficiency was promoted by his being changed from one circuit to another every year or two, Wesley established the "<a href="https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/itinerant" class="extiw" title="wiktionary:itinerant">itinerancy</a>" and insisted that his preachers submit to its rules.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESpiak2011_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESpiak2011-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>John Wesley had strong links with the <a href="/wiki/North_West_of_England" class="mw-redirect" title="North West of England">North West of England</a>, visiting <a href="/wiki/Manchester" title="Manchester">Manchester</a> on at least fifteen occasions between 1733 and 1790. In 1733 and 1738 he preached at <a href="/wiki/St_Ann%27s_Church,_Manchester" title="St Ann&#39;s Church, Manchester">St Ann's Church</a> and Salford Chapel, meeting with his friend <a href="/wiki/John_Clayton_(divine)" title="John Clayton (divine)">John Clayton</a>. In 1781 Wesley opened the chapel on <a href="/wiki/Oldham_Street" title="Oldham Street">Oldham Street</a>—part of the <a href="/wiki/Manchester_and_Salford_Wesleyan_Methodist_Mission" title="Manchester and Salford Wesleyan Methodist Mission">Manchester and Salford Wesleyan Methodist Mission</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHindle197578–79_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHindle197578–79-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> now the site of Manchester's Methodist Central Hall.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEManchester_City_Council2019_69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEManchester_City_Council2019-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Following an illness in 1748 John Wesley was nursed by a classleader and housekeeper, Grace Murray, at an orphan house in <a href="/wiki/Newcastle_upon_Tyne" title="Newcastle upon Tyne">Newcastle</a>. Taken with Grace, he invited her to travel with him to Ireland in 1749 where he believed them to be betrothed though they were never married. It has been suggested that his brother Charles Wesley objected to the engagement,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECollins2003_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECollins2003-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> though this is disputed. Subsequently, Grace married John Bennett, a preacher.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBunting1940277–278_71-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBunting1940277–278-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Ordination_of_ministers">Ordination of ministers</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Wesley&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Ordination of ministers">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Wesley_at_asbury.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0f/Wesley_at_asbury.jpg/220px-Wesley_at_asbury.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="293" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0f/Wesley_at_asbury.jpg/330px-Wesley_at_asbury.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0f/Wesley_at_asbury.jpg/440px-Wesley_at_asbury.jpg 2x" data-file-width="960" data-file-height="1280" /></a><figcaption>Life-size statue at <a href="/wiki/Asbury_Theological_Seminary" title="Asbury Theological Seminary">Asbury Theological Seminary</a> in <a href="/wiki/Wilmore,_Kentucky" title="Wilmore, Kentucky">Wilmore, Kentucky</a>, United States</figcaption></figure>
<p>As the societies multiplied, they adopted the elements of an <a href="/wiki/Ecclesiology" title="Ecclesiology">ecclesiastical system</a>. The divide between Wesley and the Church of England widened. The question of division from the Church of England was urged by some of his preachers and societies, but most strenuously opposed by his brother Charles. Wesley refused to leave the Church of England, believing that Anglicanism was "with all her blemishes, [...] nearer the Scriptural plans than any other in Europe".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThorsen200597_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThorsen200597-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> In 1745 Wesley wrote that he would make any concession which his conscience permitted, to live in peace with the clergy. He could not give up the doctrine of an inward and present salvation by faith itself; he would not stop preaching, nor dissolve the societies, nor end preaching by lay members. In the same year, in correspondence with a friend, he wrote that he believed it wrong to administer <a href="/wiki/Sacrament" title="Sacrament">sacraments</a> without having been ordained by a bishop.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETucker200884–85_72-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETucker200884–85-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>When, in 1746, Wesley read <a href="/wiki/Peter_King,_1st_Baron_King" title="Peter King, 1st Baron King">Lord King</a>'s account of the primitive church, he became convinced that <a href="/wiki/Apostolic_succession" title="Apostolic succession">apostolic succession</a> could be transmitted through not only bishops, but also <a href="/wiki/Presbyter" title="Presbyter">presbyters</a> (priests). He wrote that he was "a scriptural <i><a href="/wiki/Bishop#Terminology" title="Bishop">episkopos</a></i> as much as many men in England." Although he believed in apostolic succession, he also once called the idea of uninterrupted succession a "fable".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHolden187057–59_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolden187057–59-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Edward_Stillingfleet" title="Edward Stillingfleet">Edward Stillingfleet</a>'s <i>Irenicon</i> led him to decide that ordination (and <a href="/wiki/Holy_orders" title="Holy orders">holy orders</a>) could be valid when performed by a presbyter rather than a bishop. Nevertheless, some believe that Wesley was secretly consecrated a bishop in 1763 by <a href="/wiki/Erasmus_of_Arcadia" title="Erasmus of Arcadia">Erasmus of Arcadia</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWesleyan_Methodist_Magazine1836_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWesleyan_Methodist_Magazine1836-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> and that Wesley could not openly announce his episcopal consecration without incurring the penalty of the <a href="/wiki/Praemunire" title="Praemunire"><i>Præmunire</i> Act</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECooke1896145_75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECooke1896145-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1784, he believed he could no longer wait for the <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_London" title="Bishop of London">Bishop of London</a> to ordain someone for the American Methodists, who were without the sacraments after the <a href="/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War" title="American Revolutionary War">American War of Independence</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUMC_of_Indiana2019_76-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUMC_of_Indiana2019-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> The Church of England had been disestablished in the United States, where it had been the state church in most of the southern colonies. The Church of England had not yet appointed a United States bishop to what would become the <a href="/wiki/Episcopal_Church_(United_States)" title="Episcopal Church (United States)">Protestant Episcopal Church</a> in America. Wesley ordained <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Coke_(bishop)" title="Thomas Coke (bishop)">Thomas Coke</a> as <a href="/wiki/Superintendent_(ecclesiastical)" class="mw-redirect" title="Superintendent (ecclesiastical)">superintendent</a><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWesley1915264_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWesley1915264-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> of Methodists in the United States by the <a href="/wiki/Laying_on_of_hands" title="Laying on of hands">laying on of hands</a>, although Coke was already a priest in the Church of England. He also ordained <a href="/wiki/Richard_Whatcoat" title="Richard Whatcoat">Richard Whatcoat</a> and <a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Vasey&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Thomas Vasey (page does not exist)">Thomas Vasey</a> as presbyters; Whatcoat and Vasey sailed to America with Coke. Wesley intended that Coke and <a href="/wiki/Francis_Asbury" title="Francis Asbury">Francis Asbury</a> (whom Coke ordained as superintendent by direction of Wesley) should ordain others in the newly founded <a href="/wiki/Methodist_Episcopal_Church" title="Methodist Episcopal Church">Methodist Episcopal Church</a> in the United States. In 1787, Coke and Asbury persuaded the American Methodists to refer to them as bishops rather than superintendents,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee1810128_78-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee1810128-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> overruling Wesley's objections to the change.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWesley1915_79-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWesley1915-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>His brother, Charles, was alarmed by the ordinations and Wesley's evolving view of the matter. He begged Wesley to stop before he had "quite broken down the bridge" and not embitter his [Charles'] last moments on earth, nor "leave an indelible blot on our memory."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWesley2000b434_80-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWesley2000b434-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> Wesley replied that he had not separated from the church, nor did he intend to, but he must and would save as many souls as he could while alive, "without being careful about what may possibly be when I die."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWesley1931letter_1785b_81-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWesley1931letter_1785b-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> Although Wesley rejoiced that the Methodists in America were free, he advised his English followers to remain in the established church.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWatson199026_82-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWatson199026-82">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Doctrines.2C_theology_and_advocacy"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Doctrines,_theology_and_advocacy">Doctrines, theology and advocacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Wesley&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Doctrines, theology and advocacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Wesley_preaching_in_the_City_Chapel._Engraving_by_T._Bl_Wellcome_V0006869_(Cropped).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/John_Wesley_preaching_in_the_City_Chapel._Engraving_by_T._Bl_Wellcome_V0006869_%28Cropped%29.jpg/220px-John_Wesley_preaching_in_the_City_Chapel._Engraving_by_T._Bl_Wellcome_V0006869_%28Cropped%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/John_Wesley_preaching_in_the_City_Chapel._Engraving_by_T._Bl_Wellcome_V0006869_%28Cropped%29.jpg/330px-John_Wesley_preaching_in_the_City_Chapel._Engraving_by_T._Bl_Wellcome_V0006869_%28Cropped%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/John_Wesley_preaching_in_the_City_Chapel._Engraving_by_T._Bl_Wellcome_V0006869_%28Cropped%29.jpg/440px-John_Wesley_preaching_in_the_City_Chapel._Engraving_by_T._Bl_Wellcome_V0006869_%28Cropped%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="582" data-file-height="388" /></a><figcaption>Wesley preaching to his assistants in the City Road Chapel (now Wesley's Chapel), London. Detail of a stipple engraving by T. Blood, 1822.</figcaption></figure>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Wesleyan_theology" title="Wesleyan theology">Wesleyan theology</a></div>
<p>The 20th-century Wesleyan scholar <a href="/wiki/Albert_Outler" title="Albert Outler">Albert Outler</a> argued in his introduction to the 1964 collection <i>John Wesley</i> that Wesley developed his <a href="/wiki/Christian_theology" title="Christian theology">theology</a>  by using a method that Outler termed the <a href="/wiki/Wesleyan_Quadrilateral" title="Wesleyan Quadrilateral">Wesleyan Quadrilateral</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMellor2003_83-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMellor2003-83">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> In this method, Wesley believed that the living core of Christianity was contained in Scripture (the <a href="/wiki/Bible" title="Bible">Bible</a>), and that it was the sole foundational source of theological development. The centrality of Scripture was so important for Wesley that he called himself "<a href="/wiki/Homo_unius_libri" title="Homo unius libri">a man of one book</a>,"<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECraik1916_84-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECraik1916-84">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> although he was well-read for his day.
