However, he believed that <a href="/wiki/Doctrine" title="Doctrine">doctrine</a> had to be in keeping with Christian <a href="/wiki/Orthodoxy" title="Orthodoxy">orthodox</a> tradition. So, tradition was considered the second aspect of the Quadrilateral.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMellor2003_83-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMellor2003-83">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> Wesley contended that a part of the theological method would involve experiential faith. In other words, truth would be vivified in personal experience of Christians (overall, not individually), if it were really truth. And every doctrine must be able to be defended rationally. He did not divorce <a href="/wiki/Faith_in_Christianity" title="Faith in Christianity">faith</a> from <a href="/wiki/Reason" title="Reason">reason</a>. Tradition, experience and reason, however, were subject always to Scripture, Wesley argued, because only there is the Word of God <a href="/wiki/Revelation" title="Revelation">revealed</a> "so far as it is necessary for our salvation."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPatterson1984Article_IV_–_The_Holy_Bible_85-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPatterson1984Article_IV_–_The_Holy_Bible-85">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Wesley_at_St_Paul%27s.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/91/John_Wesley_at_St_Paul%27s.jpg/170px-John_Wesley_at_St_Paul%27s.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="305" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/91/John_Wesley_at_St_Paul%27s.jpg/255px-John_Wesley_at_St_Paul%27s.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/91/John_Wesley_at_St_Paul%27s.jpg/340px-John_Wesley_at_St_Paul%27s.jpg 2x" data-file-width="600" data-file-height="1075" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Statue_of_John_Wesley,_St_Paul%27s_Churchyard" title="Statue of John Wesley, St Paul&#39;s Churchyard">Bronze statue of Wesley in St Paul's Churchyard</a>, London. A marble version stands inside <a href="/wiki/Methodist_Central_Hall,_Westminster" title="Methodist Central Hall, Westminster">Methodist Central Hall, Westminster</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The doctrines which Wesley emphasised in his <a href="/wiki/Sermon" title="Sermon">sermons</a> and writings are <a href="/wiki/Prevenient_grace" title="Prevenient grace">prevenient grace</a>, present personal salvation by faith, the <a href="/wiki/Assurance_(theology)" title="Assurance (theology)">witness of the Spirit</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Entire_sanctification" class="mw-redirect" title="Entire sanctification">entire sanctification</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcDonald2012117_86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcDonald2012117-86">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELoyer201419_87-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELoyer201419-87">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> Prevenient grace was the theological underpinning of his belief that all persons were capable of being saved by faith in Christ. Unlike the Calvinists of his day, Wesley did not believe in <a href="/wiki/Predestination" title="Predestination">predestination</a>, that is, that some persons had been elected by God for salvation and others for damnation. He understood that Christian orthodoxy insisted that salvation was only possible by the sovereign grace of God. He expressed his understanding of humanity's relationship to God as utter dependence upon God's grace. God was at work to enable all people to be capable of coming to faith by empowering humans to have actual existential freedom of response to God.
</p><p>Wesley defined the witness of the Spirit as: "an inward impression on the soul of believers, whereby the Spirit of God directly testifies to their spirit that they are the children of God."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWesley1984296_88-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWesley1984296-88">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> He based this doctrine upon certain Biblical passages (see <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://bible.oremus.org/?passage=Romans%208:15–16&amp;version=nrsv">Romans 8:15–16</a> as an example).<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (July 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> This doctrine was closely related to his belief that salvation had to be "personal." In his view, a person must ultimately believe the <a href="/wiki/The_gospel" title="The gospel">Good News</a> for himself or herself; no one could be in relation to God for another.
</p>
<p><a href="/wiki/Entire_sanctification" class="mw-redirect" title="Entire sanctification">Entire sanctification</a> he described in 1790 as the "grand <i>depositum</i> which God has lodged with the people called 'Methodists'."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELoyer201419_87-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELoyer201419-87">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMiller2012_89-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiller2012-89">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> Wesley taught that entire sanctification was obtainable after <a href="/wiki/Justification_(theology)" title="Justification (theology)">justification</a> by faith, between justification and death.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELoyer201419_87-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELoyer201419-87">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> Wesley defined it as:</p><blockquote><p>"That habitual disposition of soul which, in the sacred writings, is termed holiness; and which directly implies, the being cleansed from sin, 'from all filthiness both of flesh and spirit;' and, by consequence, the being endued with those virtues which were in Christ Jesus; the being so 'renewed in the image of our mind,' as to be 'perfect as our Father in heaven is perfect."<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote><p>The term "sinless perfection" was one which Wesley avoided using "because of its ambiguity,"<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> rather, he contended that a Christian could be made "<a href="/wiki/Christian_perfection" title="Christian perfection">perfect in love</a>". (Wesley studied <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodoxy</a> and embraced particularly the doctrine of <i><a href="/wiki/Theosis_(Eastern_Orthodox_theology)" class="mw-redirect" title="Theosis (Eastern Orthodox theology)">Theosis</a></i>).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcCormick1991_92-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcCormick1991-92">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> This love would mean, first of all, that a believer's motives, rather than being self-centred, would be guided by the deep desire to please God. One would be able to keep from committing what Wesley called, "sin rightly so-called." By this he meant a conscious or intentional breach of God's will or laws.
</p><p>Secondly, to be made perfect in love meant, for Wesley, that a Christian could live with a primary guiding regard for others and their welfare. He based this on Christ's quote that the second great command is "to love your neighbour as you love yourself." In Wesley's view, this orientation would cause a person to avoid any number of sins against his neighbour. This love, plus the love for God that could be the central focus of a person's faith, would be what Wesley referred to as "a fulfilment of the law of Christ."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJones200233_93-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJones200233-93">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> He maintained that individuals could have assurance of perfection, akin to a second conversion or instantaneous sanctifying experience, through the testimony of the Spirit. Wesley collected and published such testimonies.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWesley176658,_62–69_94-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWesley176658,_62–69-94">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>John Wesley's statements against the <a href="/wiki/Islamic_faith" class="mw-redirect" title="Islamic faith">Islamic faith</a> are well known. Wesley assumed the superiority of Christianity vis-a-vis to Islam, based on his commitment to the biblical revelation as "the book of God". His theologic interpretation of Christianity was seeking its imperative rather than considering other <a href="/wiki/Abrahamic" class="mw-redirect" title="Abrahamic">Abrahamic</a> and <a href="/wiki/Eastern_religion" class="mw-redirect" title="Eastern religion">Eastern religions</a> to be equal. He often regarded the lifestyles of Muslims as an "ox goad" to prick the collective Christian conscience.<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Advocacy_of_Arminianism">Advocacy of Arminianism</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Wesley&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Advocacy of Arminianism">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Wesley_by_William_Hamilton.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/John_Wesley_by_William_Hamilton.jpg/220px-John_Wesley_by_William_Hamilton.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="271" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/John_Wesley_by_William_Hamilton.jpg/330px-John_Wesley_by_William_Hamilton.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/John_Wesley_by_William_Hamilton.jpg/440px-John_Wesley_by_William_Hamilton.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2125" data-file-height="2621" /></a><figcaption>Portrait of Wesley by <a href="/wiki/William_Hamilton_(painter)" title="William Hamilton (painter)">William Hamilton</a>, 1788. Currently on display in <a href="/wiki/Wesley%27s_Chapel#John_Wesley&#39;s_House" title="Wesley&#39;s Chapel">John Wesley's House</a>, City Road, London.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Wesley entered controversies as he tried to enlarge church practice. The most notable of his controversies was that on <a href="/wiki/Calvinism" title="Calvinism">Calvinism</a>. His father was of the <a href="/wiki/Arminianism_in_the_Church_of_England" title="Arminianism in the Church of England">Arminian</a> school in the church. Wesley came to his own conclusions while in college and expressed himself strongly against the doctrines of Calvinistic election and <a href="/wiki/Reprobation" title="Reprobation">reprobation</a>. His system of thought has become known as <a href="/wiki/Arminianism#Wesleyan_Arminianism" title="Arminianism">Wesleyan Arminianism</a>, the foundations of which were laid by Wesley and his fellow preacher <a href="/wiki/John_William_Fletcher" title="John William Fletcher">John William Fletcher</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKnight2018115_96-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKnight2018115-96">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> Although Wesley knew very little about the beliefs of <a href="/wiki/Jacob_Arminius" class="mw-redirect" title="Jacob Arminius">Jacob Arminius</a> and arrived at his religious views independently of Arminius, Wesley acknowledged late in life, with the 1778 publication of <i><a href="/wiki/Wesleyan_Methodist_Magazine" title="Wesleyan Methodist Magazine">The Arminian Magazine</a></i>, that he and Arminius were in general agreement. Theology Professor W. Stephen Gunther concludes he was "a faithful representative" of Arminius' beliefs.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGunter200769_97-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGunter200769-97">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> Wesley was perhaps the clearest English proponent of Arminianism.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStanglinMcCall2012153_98-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStanglinMcCall2012153-98">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>By contrast, Whitefield inclined to Calvinism; in his first tour in America, he embraced the views of the <a href="/wiki/New_England_theology" title="New England theology">New England School</a> of Calvinism. Whitefield opposed Wesley's advocacy of Arminianism, though the two maintained a strained friendship. When in 1739 Wesley preached a sermon on <i>Freedom of Grace</i>, attacking the Calvinistic understanding of <a href="/wiki/Predestination" title="Predestination">predestination</a> as blasphemous, as it represented "God as worse than the devil," Whitefield asked him not to repeat or publish the discourse, as he did not want a dispute. Wesley published his sermon anyway. Whitefield was one of many who responded. The two men separated their practice in 1741. Wesley wrote that those who held to <a href="/wiki/Unlimited_atonement" title="Unlimited atonement">unlimited atonement</a> did not desire separation, but "those who held 'particular redemption' would not hear of any accommodation."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStevens1858155_99-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStevens1858155-99">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Joseph_Badger_-_George_Whitefield_(1714-1770)_-_H27_-_Harvard_Art_Museums.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ab/Joseph_Badger_-_George_Whitefield_%281714-1770%29_-_H27_-_Harvard_Art_Museums.jpg/170px-Joseph_Badger_-_George_Whitefield_%281714-1770%29_-_H27_-_Harvard_Art_Museums.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="217" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ab/Joseph_Badger_-_George_Whitefield_%281714-1770%29_-_H27_-_Harvard_Art_Museums.jpg/255px-Joseph_Badger_-_George_Whitefield_%281714-1770%29_-_H27_-_Harvard_Art_Museums.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ab/Joseph_Badger_-_George_Whitefield_%281714-1770%29_-_H27_-_Harvard_Art_Museums.jpg/340px-Joseph_Badger_-_George_Whitefield_%281714-1770%29_-_H27_-_Harvard_Art_Museums.jpg 2x" data-file-width="803" data-file-height="1024" /></a><figcaption><i><a href="/wiki/George_Whitefield" title="George Whitefield">George Whitefield</a></i> by <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Badger" title="Joseph Badger">Joseph Badger</a>, c. 1745 (<a href="/wiki/Harvard_University" title="Harvard University">Harvard University</a> Portrait Collection)</figcaption></figure>
<p>Whitefield, <a href="/wiki/Howell_Harris" title="Howell Harris">Howell Harris</a> (leader of the <a href="/wiki/Welsh_Methodist_revival" title="Welsh Methodist revival">Welsh Methodist revival</a>),<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEvans1961_100-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans1961-100">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/John_Cennick" title="John Cennick">John Cennick</a>, and others, became the founders of <a href="/wiki/Presbyterian_Church_of_Wales" title="Presbyterian Church of Wales">Calvinistic Methodism</a>. Whitefield and Wesley, however, were soon back on friendly terms, and their friendship remained unbroken although they travelled different paths. When someone asked Whitefield if he thought he would see Wesley in heaven, Whitefield replied, "I fear not, for he will be so near the eternal throne and we at such a distance, we shall hardly get sight of him."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWiersbe1984255_101-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWiersbe1984255-101">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1770, the controversy broke out anew with violence and bitterness, as people's view of God related to their views of men and their possibilities. <a href="/wiki/Augustus_Toplady" title="Augustus Toplady">Augustus Toplady</a>, <a href="/wiki/Daniel_Rowland_(preacher)" title="Daniel Rowland (preacher)">Daniel Rowland</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sir_Richard_Hill,_2nd_Baronet" title="Sir Richard Hill, 2nd Baronet">Sir Richard Hill</a> and others were engaged on one side, while Wesley and Fletcher stood on the other. Toplady was editor of <i><a href="/wiki/The_Gospel_Magazine" title="The Gospel Magazine">The Gospel Magazine</a></i>, which had articles covering the controversy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETyerman187656_102-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETyerman187656-102">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1778, Wesley began the publication of <i><a href="/wiki/Wesleyan_Methodist_Magazine" title="Wesleyan Methodist Magazine">The Arminian Magazine</a></i>, not, he said, to convince Calvinists, but to preserve Methodists. He wanted to teach the truth that "God willeth all men to be saved."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGunter20114_103-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGunter20114-103">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> A "lasting peace" could be secured in no other way.
</p><p>Some have suggested that later in life Wesley may have embraced the doctrine of <a href="/wiki/Universal_salvation" class="mw-redirect" title="Universal salvation">universal salvation</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> although this is disputed. The claim is supported by a letter Wesley wrote in 1787 in which he endorsed as "one of the most sensible tracts I have ever read"<sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> a work by <a href="/wiki/Charles_Bonnet" title="Charles Bonnet">Charles Bonnet</a> in which the author's conclusion was, "There will therefore be a perpetual advance of all the individuals of humanity towards greater perfection or greater happiness."<sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Support_for_abolitionism">Support for abolitionism</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Wesley&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Support for abolitionism">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Later in his ministry, Wesley was a keen <a href="/wiki/Abolitionism" title="Abolitionism">abolitionist</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECarey2005145–151_108-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECarey2005145–151-108">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECarey2003_109-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECarey2003-109">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup> speaking out and writing against the <a href="/wiki/Slave_trade" class="mw-redirect" title="Slave trade">slave trade</a>. Wesley denounced slavery as "the sum of all villainies" and detailed its abuses.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWesley1774_110-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWesley1774-110">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> He addressed the slave trade in a polemical <a href="/wiki/Tract_(literature)" title="Tract (literature)">tract</a>, titled <i>Thoughts Upon Slavery,</i> in 1774.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWesley1774_110-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWesley1774-110">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEField20153_111-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEField20153-111">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> He wrote, "Liberty is the right of every human creature, as soon as he breathes the vital air; and no human law can deprive him of that right which he derives from the law of nature".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEYrigoyen1996_112-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEYrigoyen1996-112">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup> Wesley influenced George Whitefield to journey to the colonies, spurring the transatlantic debate on slavery.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEYoon2012_113-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEYoon2012-113">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> Wesley was a mentor to <a href="/wiki/William_Wilberforce" title="William Wilberforce">William Wilberforce</a>, who was also influential in the abolition of slavery in the British Empire.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEField20156_114-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEField20156-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>It is thanks to Wesley's abolitionist message that a young <a href="/wiki/African_American" class="mw-redirect" title="African American">African American</a>, <a href="/wiki/Richard_Allen_(bishop)" title="Richard Allen (bishop)">Richard Allen</a>, converted to Christianity in 1777 and later founded, in 1816, the <a href="/wiki/African_Methodist_Episcopal_Church" title="African Methodist Episcopal Church">African Methodist Episcopal Church</a> (AME), in the Methodist tradition.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAnon194162_115-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAnon194162-115">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-EB2020_116-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EB2020-116">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Support_for_women_preachers">Support for women preachers</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Wesley&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Support for women preachers">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Women had an active role in Wesley's Methodism, and were encouraged to lead classes. In 1761, he informally allowed <a href="/wiki/Sarah_Crosby" title="Sarah Crosby">Sarah Crosby</a>, one of his converts and a class leader, to preach.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEnglish1994_117-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEnglish1994-117">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup> On an occasion where over 200 people attended a class she was meant to teach, Crosby felt as though she could not fulfill her duties as a class leader given the large crowd, and decided to preach instead.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBurge19969_118-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurge19969-118">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJensen2013120_119-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJensen2013120-119">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup> She wrote to Wesley to seek his advice and forgiveness.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELloyd200933_120-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELloyd200933-120">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup> He let Crosby to continue her preaching so long as she refrained from as many of the mannerisms of preaching as she could.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChilcote1991121–122_121-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChilcote1991121–122-121">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> Between 1761 and 1771, Wesley wrote detailed instructions to Crosby and others, with specifics on what styles of preaching they could use. For instance, in 1769, Wesley allowed Crosby to give exhortations.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETuckerLiefeld2010241_122-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETuckerLiefeld2010241-122">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the summer of 1771, <a href="/wiki/Mary_Bosanquet_Fletcher" class="mw-redirect" title="Mary Bosanquet Fletcher">Mary Bosanquet</a> wrote to John Wesley to defend hers and Sarah Crosby's work preaching and leading classes at her orphanage, Cross Hall.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChilcote199378_123-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChilcote199378-123">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBurton2008164_124-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurton2008164-124">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup> Bosanquet's letter is considered to be the first full and true defense of women's preaching in Methodism.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChilcote199378_123-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChilcote199378-123">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> Her argument was that women should be able to preach when they experienced an 'extraordinary call,' or when given permission from God.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChilcote199378_123-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChilcote199378-123">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELloyd200934_125-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELloyd200934-125">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> Wesley accepted Bosanquet's argument, and formally began to allow women to preach in Methodism in 1771.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEason200378_126-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEason200378-126">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELloyd200934_125-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELloyd200934-125">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> Methodist women, including preachers, continued to observe the ancient practice of <a href="/wiki/Christian_head_covering" title="Christian head covering">Christian head covering</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Dunlap1994_127-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Dunlap1994-127">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Personality_and_activities">Personality and activities</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Wesley&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Personality and activities">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Electrical_machine_designed_by_John_Wesley,_18th_c._Wellcome_L0003006.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9e/Electrical_machine_designed_by_John_Wesley%2C_18th_c._Wellcome_L0003006.jpg/150px-Electrical_machine_designed_by_John_Wesley%2C_18th_c._Wellcome_L0003006.jpg" decoding="async" width="150" height="172" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9e/Electrical_machine_designed_by_John_Wesley%2C_18th_c._Wellcome_L0003006.jpg/225px-Electrical_machine_designed_by_John_Wesley%2C_18th_c._Wellcome_L0003006.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9e/Electrical_machine_designed_by_John_Wesley%2C_18th_c._Wellcome_L0003006.jpg/300px-Electrical_machine_designed_by_John_Wesley%2C_18th_c._Wellcome_L0003006.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4025" data-file-height="4625" /></a><figcaption>Sketch of an electrical machine designed by Wesley</figcaption></figure>
<p>Wesley <a href="/wiki/Itinerant_preacher" title="Itinerant preacher">travelled widely</a>, generally on horseback, preaching two or three times each day. Stephen Tomkins writes that "[Wesley] rode 250,000 miles, gave away 30,000 pounds, ... and preached more than 40,000 sermons... "<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOakes2004_128-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOakes2004-128">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup> He formed societies, opened chapels, examined and commissioned preachers, administered aid charities, prescribed for the sick, helped to pioneer the use of <a href="/wiki/Electroconvulsive_therapy" title="Electroconvulsive therapy">electric shock</a> for the treatment of illness,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJohnstone2000152_129-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJohnstone2000152-129">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup> and superintended <a href="/wiki/Orphanage" title="Orphanage">orphanages</a> and schools (including <a href="/wiki/Kingswood_School" title="Kingswood School">Kingswood School</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERyan2017_130-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERyan2017-130">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Wesley practised a <a href="/wiki/Christian_vegetarianism" title="Christian vegetarianism">vegetarian</a> diet and in later life abstained from wine for health reasons.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPreece2008239_131-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPreece2008239-131">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> He wrote, "thanks be to God, since the time I gave up flesh meals and wine I have been delivered from all physical ills." Wesley warned against the dangers of <a href="/wiki/Alcohol_abuse" title="Alcohol abuse">alcohol abuse</a> in his famous sermon, <i>The Use of Money,</i><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWesleyJackson1979Sermon_#50:_The_Use_Of_Money_132-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWesleyJackson1979Sermon_#50:_The_Use_Of_Money-132">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> and in his letter to an alcoholic.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAnderson2001_133-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAnderson2001-133">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> In his sermon, <i>On Public Diversions</i>, Wesley says: "You see the wine when it sparkles in the cup, and are going to drink of it. I tell you there is poison in it! and, therefore, beg you to throw it away".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWesleyJackson1979Sermon_#140:_On_Public_Diversions_134-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWesleyJackson1979Sermon_#140:_On_Public_Diversions-134">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup> These statements against alcohol use largely concerned "hard liquors and spirits" rather than the low-alcohol beer, which was often safer to drink than the contaminated water of that time.<sup id="cite_ref-135" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-135">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup> Methodist churches became pioneers in the teetotal <a href="/wiki/Temperance_movement" title="Temperance movement">temperance movement</a> of the 19th and 20th centuries, and later it became <i>de rigueur</i> in British Methodism.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECurtis2016_136-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis2016-136">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He attended music concerts, and was especially an admirer of <a href="/wiki/Charles_Avison" title="Charles Avison">Charles Avison</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFay2017_137-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFay2017-137">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup> After attending a performance in Bristol Cathedral in 1758, Wesley recorded in his journal: "I went to the cathedral to hear <a href="/wiki/George_Frideric_Handel" title="George Frideric Handel">Mr. Handel's</a> <i><a href="/wiki/Messiah_(Handel)" title="Messiah (Handel)">Messiah</a></i>. I doubt if that congregation was ever so serious at a sermon as they were during this performance. In many places, especially several of the choruses, it exceeded my expectation."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWesley18357_138-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWesley18357-138">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Wesley._Stipple_engraving_by_F._Bartolozzi_after_J._Zof_Wellcome_V0006236.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a4/John_Wesley._Stipple_engraving_by_F._Bartolozzi_after_J._Zof_Wellcome_V0006236.jpg/220px-John_Wesley._Stipple_engraving_by_F._Bartolozzi_after_J._Zof_Wellcome_V0006236.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="269" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a4/John_Wesley._Stipple_engraving_by_F._Bartolozzi_after_J._Zof_Wellcome_V0006236.jpg/330px-John_Wesley._Stipple_engraving_by_F._Bartolozzi_after_J._Zof_Wellcome_V0006236.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a4/John_Wesley._Stipple_engraving_by_F._Bartolozzi_after_J._Zof_Wellcome_V0006236.jpg/440px-John_Wesley._Stipple_engraving_by_F._Bartolozzi_after_J._Zof_Wellcome_V0006236.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2398" data-file-height="2928" /></a><figcaption>Stipple engraving by <a href="/wiki/Francesco_Bartolozzi" title="Francesco Bartolozzi">Francesco Bartolozzi</a>, 1760</figcaption></figure>
<p>He is described as "rather under the medium height, well proportioned, strong, with a bright eye, a clear complexion, and a saintly, intellectual face".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECarroll1953310_139-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECarroll1953310-139">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup> Though Wesley favoured celibacy rather than marital bond,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECoe1996_140-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECoe1996-140">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELawrence2011134–136_141-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELawrence2011134–136-141">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup> he married very unhappily in 1751, at the age of 48, to a widow, Mary Vazeille, described as "a well-to-do widow and mother of four children."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBusenitz2013_142-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBusenitz2013-142">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup> The couple had no children. John Singleton writes: "By 1758 she had left him—unable to cope, it is said, with the competition for his time and devotion presented by the ever-burgeoning Methodist movement. Molly, as she was known, was to return and leave him again on several occasions before their final separation."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBusenitz2013_142-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBusenitz2013-142">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup> Wesley wryly reported in his journal, "I did not forsake her, I did not dismiss her, I will not recall her."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESouthey1820137_143-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESouthey1820137-143">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1770, at the death of George Whitefield, Wesley wrote a memorial sermon which praised Whitefield's admirable qualities and acknowledged the two men's differences: "There are many doctrines of a less essential nature ... In these we may think and let think; we may '<a href="/wiki/Agree_to_disagree" title="Agree to disagree">agree to disagree</a>.' But, meantime, let us hold fast the essentials..."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWesleyJackson1979Sermon_#53:_On_The_Death_of_The_Rev._Mr._George_Whitefield_144-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWesleyJackson1979Sermon_#53:_On_The_Death_of_The_Rev._Mr._George_Whitefield-144">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup> Wesley may have been the first to use "agree to disagree" in print—in the modern sense of tolerating differences—though he himself attributed the saying to Whitefield, and it had appeared in other senses previously.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMartin2019_145-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartin2019-145">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Wesley was gravely ill during a visit to <a href="/wiki/Lisburn" title="Lisburn">Lisburn</a> in Ireland in June 1775. He stayed at the home of a leading Methodist, <a href="/wiki/Henrietta_Gayer" title="Henrietta Gayer">Henrietta Gayer</a>, where it was thought he would die. Prayers were said "for another fifteen years" and he recovered.<sup id="cite_ref-146" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-146">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Death">Death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Wesley&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:The_death-bed_of_John_Wesley,_1791._Process_print_after_an_a_Wellcome_V0006951.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d0/The_death-bed_of_John_Wesley%2C_1791._Process_print_after_an_a_Wellcome_V0006951.jpg/220px-The_death-bed_of_John_Wesley%2C_1791._Process_print_after_an_a_Wellcome_V0006951.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="162" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d0/The_death-bed_of_John_Wesley%2C_1791._Process_print_after_an_a_Wellcome_V0006951.jpg/330px-The_death-bed_of_John_Wesley%2C_1791._Process_print_after_an_a_Wellcome_V0006951.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d0/The_death-bed_of_John_Wesley%2C_1791._Process_print_after_an_a_Wellcome_V0006951.jpg/440px-The_death-bed_of_John_Wesley%2C_1791._Process_print_after_an_a_Wellcome_V0006951.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1938" data-file-height="1423" /></a><figcaption>Wesley on his deathbed: "The best of all is, God is with us". Mezzotint by <a href="/wiki/John_Sartain" title="John Sartain">John Sartain</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Wesley's health declined sharply towards the end of his life and he ceased preaching. On 28 June 1790, less than a year before his death, he wrote:
</p>
<blockquote><p>This day I enter into my eighty-eighth year. For above eighty-six years, I found none of the infirmities of old age: my eyes did not wax dim, neither was my natural strength abated. But last August, I found almost a sudden change. My eyes were so dim that no glasses would help me. My strength likewise now quite forsook me and probably will not return in this world.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilliams2012_147-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams2012-147">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Wesley%27s_Death_Mask.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dc/John_Wesley%27s_Death_Mask.jpg/220px-John_Wesley%27s_Death_Mask.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dc/John_Wesley%27s_Death_Mask.jpg/330px-John_Wesley%27s_Death_Mask.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dc/John_Wesley%27s_Death_Mask.jpg/440px-John_Wesley%27s_Death_Mask.jpg 2x" data-file-width="640" data-file-height="480" /></a><figcaption>Wesley's <a href="/wiki/Death_mask" title="Death mask">death mask</a>, exhibited in the <a href="/wiki/Wesley%27s_Chapel" title="Wesley&#39;s Chapel">Museum of Methodism</a>, London</figcaption></figure>
<p>Wesley was cared for during his last months by <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_Ritchie" title="Elizabeth Ritchie">Elizabeth Ritchie</a> and his physician John Whitehead. He died on 2 March 1791, at the age of 87.<sup id="cite_ref-rit_148-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-rit-148">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup> As he lay dying, his friends gathered around him, Wesley grasped their hands and said repeatedly, "Farewell, farewell." At the end, he said, "The best of all is, God is with us", lifted his arms and raised his feeble voice again, repeating the words, "The best of all is, God is with us."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHurst1903298_149-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHurst1903298-149">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup> He was entombed at his chapel on City Road, London.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHistoric_England_150-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHistoric_England-150">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup> Ritchie wrote an account of his death which was quoted from by Whitehead at  his funeral.<sup id="cite_ref-rit_148-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-rit-148">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Because of his charitable nature he died poor, leaving as the result of his life's work 135,000 members and 541 itinerant preachers under the name "Methodist". It has been said that "when John Wesley was carried to his grave, he left behind him a good library of books, a well-worn clergyman's gown" and the Methodist Church.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHurst1903298_149-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHurst1903298-149">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Literary_work">Literary work</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Wesley&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Literary work">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/John_Wesley_bibliography" title="John Wesley bibliography">John Wesley bibliography</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Wesley%27s_shorthand_writing.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/John_Wesley%27s_shorthand_writing.jpg/220px-John_Wesley%27s_shorthand_writing.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="150" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/John_Wesley%27s_shorthand_writing.jpg/330px-John_Wesley%27s_shorthand_writing.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/John_Wesley%27s_shorthand_writing.jpg/440px-John_Wesley%27s_shorthand_writing.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1172" data-file-height="800" /></a><figcaption>Wesley's <a href="/wiki/Shorthand" title="Shorthand">shorthand</a> writing</figcaption></figure>
<p>Wesley wrote, edited or abridged some 400 publications. As well as theology he wrote about music, marriage, medicine, <a href="/wiki/Abolitionism" title="Abolitionism">abolitionism</a> and politics.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThe_Methodist_Church2019_151-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_Methodist_Church2019-151">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup> Wesley was a logical thinker and expressed himself clearly, concisely and forcefully in writing. Between 1746 and 1760, Wesley compiled several volumes of written sermons, published as <i><a href="/wiki/Sermons_on_Several_Occasions" class="mw-redirect" title="Sermons on Several Occasions">Sermons on Several Occasions</a></i>; the first four volumes comprise forty-four sermons which are <a href="/wiki/Doctrinal" class="mw-redirect" title="Doctrinal">doctrinal</a> in content.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWesley2015134–135_152-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWesley2015134–135-152">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup> His <i>Forty-Four Sermons</i> and the <i><a href="/wiki/Explanatory_Notes_Upon_the_New_Testament" title="Explanatory Notes Upon the New Testament">Explanatory Notes Upon the New Testament</a></i> (1755) are Methodist doctrinal standards.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThe_Methodist_Church2020213_153-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_Methodist_Church2020213-153">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup> Wesley was a fluent, powerful and effective preacher; he usually preached spontaneously and briefly, though occasionally at great length.
</p><p>In his <i>Christian Library</i> (1750), he writes about <a href="/wiki/List_of_Christian_mystics" title="List of Christian mystics">mystics</a> such as <a href="/wiki/Macarius_of_Egypt" title="Macarius of Egypt">Macarius of Egypt</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ephrem_the_Syrian" title="Ephrem the Syrian">Ephrem the Syrian</a>, <a href="/wiki/Madame_Guyon" class="mw-redirect" title="Madame Guyon">Madame Guyon</a>, <a href="/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_F%C3%A9nelon" title="François Fénelon">François Fénelon</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ignatius_of_Loyola" title="Ignatius of Loyola">Ignatius of Loyola</a>, <a href="/wiki/John_of_%C3%81vila" title="John of Ávila">John of Ávila</a>, <a href="/wiki/Francis_de_Sales" title="Francis de Sales">Francis de Sales</a>, <a href="/wiki/Blaise_Pascal" title="Blaise Pascal">Blaise Pascal</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Antoinette_Bourignon" title="Antoinette Bourignon">Antoinette Bourignon</a>. The work reflects the influence of <a href="/wiki/Christian_mysticism" title="Christian mysticism">Christian mysticism</a> in Wesley's ministry from the beginning to the end,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWesleyWhaling198110_13-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWesleyWhaling198110-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> although he ever rejected it after the failure in Georgia mission.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHeath201060–61_154-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeath201060–61-154">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Wesley's <a href="/wiki/Prose" title="Prose">prose</a>, <i>Works</i>, were first collected by himself (32 vols., Bristol, 1771–74, frequently reprinted in editions varying greatly in the number of volumes). His chief prose works are a standard publication in seven octavo volumes of the Methodist Book Concern, New York. The <i>Poetical Works</i> of John and Charles, ed. G. Osborn, appeared in 13 vols., London, 1868–72.
</p><p>In addition to his <i>Sermons</i> and <i>Notes</i> are his <i>Journals</i> (originally published in 20 parts, London, 1740–89; new ed. by N. Curnock containing notes from unpublished diaries, 6 vols., vols. i–ii, London and New York, 1909–11); <i>The Doctrine of Original Sin</i> (Bristol, 1757; in reply to <a href="/wiki/John_Taylor_(dissenting_preacher)" title="John Taylor (dissenting preacher)">John Taylor</a> of Norwich); <i>An Earnest Appeal to Men of Reason and Religion</i> (originally published in three parts; 2nd ed., Bristol, 1743), an elaborate defence of Methodism, describing the evils of the times in society and the church; and a <i>Plain Account of Christian Perfection</i> (1766).
</p><p>Wesley's <i><a href="/wiki/The_Sunday_Service_of_the_Methodists" title="The Sunday Service of the Methodists">Sunday Service</a></i> was an adaptation of the <i><a href="/wiki/Book_of_Common_Prayer" title="Book of Common Prayer">Book of Common Prayer</a></i> for use by <a href="/wiki/History_of_Methodism_in_the_United_States" title="History of Methodism in the United States">American Methodists</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETucker1996_155-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETucker1996-155">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup> In his <a href="/wiki/Watchnight_service" title="Watchnight service">Watchnight service</a>, he made use of a <a href="/wiki/Pietist" class="mw-redirect" title="Pietist">pietist</a> prayer now generally known as the <a href="/wiki/Wesley_Covenant_Prayer" class="mw-redirect" title="Wesley Covenant Prayer">Wesley Covenant Prayer</a>, perhaps his most famous contribution to Christian liturgy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEParkes1997_156-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEParkes1997-156">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup> He was a noted hymn-writer, translator and compiler of a hymnal.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWesley1779_157-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWesley1779-157">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Wesley also wrote on physics and medicine, such as in <a href="/wiki/The_Desideratum;_or,_Electricity_Made_Plain_and_Useful" title="The Desideratum; or, Electricity Made Plain and Useful"><i>The Desideratum</i></a>, subtitled <i>Electricity made Plain and Useful by a Lover of Mankind and of Common Sense</i> (1759).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWesley1759_158-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWesley1759-158">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup> and <i>Primitive Physic, Or, An Easy and Natural Method of Curing Most Diseases</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWesley1744_159-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWesley1744-159">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In spite of the proliferation of his literary output, Wesley was challenged for <a href="/wiki/Plagiarism" title="Plagiarism">plagiarism</a>, for borrowing heavily from an essay by <a href="/wiki/Samuel_Johnson" title="Samuel Johnson">Samuel Johnson</a>, published in March 1775. Initially denying the charge, Wesley later apologised officially.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAbelove1997_160-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbelove1997-160">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Commemoration_and_legacy">Commemoration and legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Wesley&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Commemoration and legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Wesley continues to be the primary theological influence on Methodists and Methodist-heritage groups the world over; the Methodist movement numbers 75 million adherents in more than 130 countries.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECracknellWhite2005vii_161-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECracknellWhite2005vii-161">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Wesleyan" class="mw-redirect" title="Wesleyan">Wesleyan</a> teachings also serve as a basis for the <a href="/wiki/Holiness_movement" title="Holiness movement">Holiness movement</a>, which includes denominations like <a href="/wiki/Free_Methodist_Church" title="Free Methodist Church">Free Methodist Church</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_the_Nazarene" title="Church of the Nazarene">Church of the Nazarene</a>, <a href="/wiki/The_Salvation_Army" title="The Salvation Army">the Salvation Army</a>, and several smaller groups, and from which <a href="/wiki/Pentecostalism" title="Pentecostalism">Pentecostalism</a> and parts of the <a href="/wiki/Charismatic_movement" title="Charismatic movement">Charismatic movement</a> are offshoots.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDayton198748_162-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDayton198748-162">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup> Wesley's call to personal and social holiness continues to challenge Christians who attempt to discern what it means to participate in the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_God" class="mw-redirect" title="Kingdom of God">Kingdom of God</a>.
</p><p>He is commemorated in the <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Lutheran)" title="Calendar of saints (Lutheran)">Calendar of Saints</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Evangelical_Lutheran_Church_in_America" title="Evangelical Lutheran Church in America">Evangelical Lutheran Church in America</a> on 2 March with his brother <a href="/wiki/Charles_Wesley" title="Charles Wesley">Charles</a>. The Wesley brothers are <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints" title="Calendar of saints">honoured</a><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAnon.2010257_163-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAnon.2010257-163">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup> with a <a href="/wiki/Lesser_Feasts_and_Fasts" class="mw-redirect" title="Lesser Feasts and Fasts">Lesser Feast</a> on 3 March in the <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Episcopal_Church)" title="Calendar of saints (Episcopal Church)">Calendar of Saints</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Episcopal_Church_(United_States)" title="Episcopal Church (United States)">Episcopal Church</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECampbell201989_164-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECampbell201989-164">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup> and on 24 May (<a href="/wiki/Aldersgate_Day" title="Aldersgate Day">Aldersgate Day</a>, with a <a href="/wiki/Lesser_Festival_(Anglicanism)" title="Lesser Festival (Anglicanism)">Lesser Festival</a>) in the Church of England's <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Church_of_England)" title="Calendar of saints (Church of England)">Calendar</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBuchanan2006470_165-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBuchanan2006470-165">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2002, Wesley was listed at number 50 on the BBC's list of the <a href="/wiki/100_Greatest_Britons" title="100 Greatest Britons">100 Greatest Britons</a>, drawn from a poll of the British public.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBBC_News2002_166-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBBC_News2002-166">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Wesley%27s_Chapel" title="Wesley&#39;s Chapel">Wesley's house and chapel</a>, which he built in 1778 on City Road in London, are still intact today and the chapel has a thriving congregation with regular services as well as the Museum of Methodism in the <a href="/wiki/Crypt" title="Crypt">crypt</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWesley&#39;s_Chapel2017_167-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWesley&#39;s_Chapel2017-167">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Numerous schools, colleges, hospitals and other institutions are named after Wesley; additionally, many are named after Methodism. In 1831, <a href="/wiki/Wesleyan_University" title="Wesleyan University">Wesleyan University</a> in <a href="/wiki/Middletown,_Connecticut" title="Middletown, Connecticut">Middletown, Connecticut</a>, was the first institution of higher education in the United States to be named after Wesley. The now secular institution was founded as an all-male Methodist college.
</p><p>A replica of the rectory where Wesley lived as a boy was built in the 1990s at <a href="/wiki/Lake_Junaluska,_North_Carolina" title="Lake Junaluska, North Carolina">Lake Junaluska, North Carolina</a>. This was an addition to a group of buildings built starting in the 1950s for the <a href="/wiki/World_Methodist_Council" title="World Methodist Council">World Methodist Council</a>, including a museum which housed letters written by Wesley<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHyatt2021_168-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHyatt2021-168">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup> and a pulpit Wesley used.<sup id="cite_ref-100_years_169-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100_years-169">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup> The museum was already having difficulty staying open, and the <a href="/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic" title="COVID-19 pandemic">COVID-19 pandemic</a> finally made closing the museum necessary. Its contents went to Bridwell Library of <a href="/wiki/Perkins_School_of_Theology" title="Perkins School of Theology">Perkins School of Theology</a>, <a href="/wiki/Southern_Methodist_University" title="Southern Methodist University">Southern Methodist University</a> in <a href="/wiki/Dallas,_Texas" class="mw-redirect" title="Dallas, Texas">Dallas, Texas</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHyatt2021_168-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHyatt2021-168">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="In_film">In film</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Wesley&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: In film">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1954, the Radio and Film Commission of the <a href="/wiki/Methodist_Church_of_Great_Britain" title="Methodist Church of Great Britain">British Methodist Church</a>, in co-operation with <a href="/wiki/J._Arthur_Rank" title="J. Arthur Rank">J. Arthur Rank</a>, produced the film <i><a href="/wiki/John_Wesley_(film)" title="John Wesley (film)">John Wesley</a></i>. This was a live-action re-telling of the story of the life of Wesley, with <a href="/wiki/Leonard_Sachs" title="Leonard Sachs">Leonard Sachs</a> in the title role.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECheetham200330_170-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECheetham200330-170">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2009, a more ambitious feature film, <i><a href="/wiki/Wesley_(film)" title="Wesley (film)">Wesley</a></i>, was released by Foundery Pictures, starring <a href="/wiki/Burgess_Jenkins" title="Burgess Jenkins">Burgess Jenkins</a> as Wesley. The film was directed by the award-winning filmmaker John Jackman.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEIMDb2019_171-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEIMDb2019-171">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="In_musical_theatre">In musical theatre</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Wesley&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: In musical theatre">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1976 the musical <i>Ride! Ride!</i>, composed by <a href="/wiki/Penelope_Thwaites" title="Penelope Thwaites">Penelope Thwaites</a> <a href="/wiki/Member_of_the_Order_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Member of the Order of Australia">AM</a> and written by Alan Thornhill, premiered at the <a href="/wiki/Westminster_Theatre" title="Westminster Theatre">Westminster Theatre</a> in London's <a href="/wiki/West_End_theatre" title="West End theatre">West End</a>. The piece is based on the true story of eighteen-year-old <a href="/wiki/Martha_Thompson" title="Martha Thompson">Martha Thompson</a>'s incarceration in <a href="/wiki/Bethlem_Royal_Hospital" title="Bethlem Royal Hospital">Bedlam</a>, an incident in Wesley's life. The premier ran for 76 performances.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThe_Guide_to_Musical_Theatre2014_172-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_Guide_to_Musical_Theatre2014-172">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup> Since then it has had more than 40 productions, both amateur and professional, including a 1999 concert version, issued on the Somm record label, with <a href="/wiki/Keith_Michell" title="Keith Michell">Keith Michell</a> as Wesley.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThwaites2000_173-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThwaites2000-173">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<li id="cite_note-FOOTNOTEWesleyan_Methodist_Magazine1836-74"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWesleyan_Methodist_Magazine1836_74-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFWesleyan_Methodist_Magazine1836">Wesleyan Methodist Magazine 1836</a>. Mr. Wesley thus became a Bishop, and consecrated Dr. Coke, who united himself with ... who gave it under his own hand that Erasmus was Bishop of Arcadia,&#160;...</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-FOOTNOTECooke1896145-75"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-FOOTNOTECooke1896145_75-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFCooke1896">Cooke 1896</a>, p.&#160;145.&#160;Dr. Peters was present at the interview, and went with and introduced Dr. Seabury to Mr. Wesley, who was so far satisfied that he would have been willingly consecrated by him in Mr. Wesley would have signed his letter of orders as bishop, which Mr. Wesley could not do without incurring the penalty of the <i>Præmunire</i> Act.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-FOOTNOTEUMC_of_Indiana2019-76"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUMC_of_Indiana2019_76-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFUMC_of_Indiana2019">UMC of Indiana 2019</a>.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-FOOTNOTEWesley1915264-77"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWesley1915264_77-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFWesley1915">Wesley 1915</a>, p.&#160;264.&#160;I have accordingly appointed Dr. Coke and Mr. Francis Asbury to be joint superintendents over our brethren in North America...</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-FOOTNOTELee1810128-78"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-FOOTNOTELee1810128_78-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFLee1810">Lee 1810</a>, p.&#160;128. This was the first time that our superintendents ever gave themselves the title of Bishops in the minutes. They changed the title themselves without the consent of the conference; and at the next conference they asked the preachers if the word Bishop might stand in the minutes; seeing that it was a scripture name, and the meaning of the word Bishop, was the same with that of Superintendent. Some of the preachers opposed the alteration... but a majority of the preachers agreed to let the word Bishop remain.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-FOOTNOTEWesley1915-79"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWesley1915_79-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFWesley1915">Wesley 1915</a>. How can you, how dare you, suffer yourself to be called Bishop? I shudder, I start at the very thought! Men may call me a knave or a fool, a rascal, a scoundrel, and I am content; but they shall never, by my consent, call me Bishop! For my sake, for God's sake, for Christ's sake, put a full end to this!</span>
</li>

<li id="cite_note-Dunlap1994-127"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Dunlap1994_127-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFDunlap1994" class="citation web cs1">Dunlap, David (1 November 1994). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://www.uplook.org/1994/11/headcovering-a-historical-perspective/">"Headcovering-A Historical Perspective"</a>. Uplook Ministries<span class="reference-accessdate">. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">24 June</span> 2019</span>. <q>Although women were allowed to preach in the Methodist ministry, the veil covering a woman's head was required as a sign of her headship to Christ. Concerning the theological significance of the veil, Wesley wrote, "For a man indeed ought not to veil his head because he is the image and glory of God in the dominion he bears over the creation, representing the supreme dominion of God, which is his glory. But the woman is a matter of glory to the man, who has a becoming dominion over her. Therefore she ought not to appear except with her head veiled as a tacit acknowledgement of it."<span class="cs1-kern-right"></span></q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=unknown&amp;rft.btitle=Headcovering-A+Historical+Perspective&amp;rft.pub=Uplook+Ministries&amp;rft.date=1994-11-01&amp;rft.aulast=Dunlap&amp;rft.aufirst=David&amp;rft_id=http%3A%2F%2Fwww.uplook.org%2F1994%2F11%2Fheadcovering-a-historical-perspective%2F&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AJohn+Wesley" class="Z3988"></span></span>
</li>
